ROBERT J. LARKIN
1912 - 1982
Bob was our well-respected and much loved Town Counsel for thirty-five years.
His able guidance, sense of humor, and wisdom was a source of inspiration to
all who served with him. As a Lunenburg resident for over forty years, his love
and dedicated service to his Town is remembered with love and appreciation. As
stated in the 1981 Annual Town Report "The docket will forever show his accom-
plishments on the Town's behalf."

Board of Selectmen
JAMES T. VAN BUREN
ANN P. HALL
AUGUST N.MISNER
VIVIA'
M
Board of Selectmen: Jamès T. Van Buren, Chairman Ann P. Hall and August N. Misner.
Increased costs, limited finances, legal and regulatory complexity -- these are the foes the Board of Select-
men wrestle on Monday nights. The tangled skein of the law and regulations complicate the duties of all our Town
officials, but most of all the burden falls on the Chief Executive Office. Add to this the double-edged sword of
liability and a litigious society and seemingly routine actions become subject to extensive discussion and legal
review.
The consequences are longer hours for the Selectmen, greater demands on the staff for research and paper
work, and an extraordinary demand on Town Counsel. In 1982 the Board met for 155 hours in regularly sched-
uled and special meetings. Town Counsel was present for 119 hours at 34 of those meetings. Additionally, at least
an equal number of hours is spent in preparation for meetings, not to mention another 47 outside meetings attend-
ed by Selectmen and weekly warrants to be signed on non-meeting days. The Board feels it is necessary to call
attention to these facts, since they provide the background for some proposals for management changes and
future study which are being prepared for your consideration at the Annual Town Meeting.
Management efforts of past Boards of Selectmen wisely began the process of reaching out beyond the com-
munity in order to provide the best services at the lowest possible cost to the taxpayers. Formal agreements such
as Mutual Aid for Police and Fire, Membership in the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health, and the Bi-Town
Building Inspector Board continue to make available increased services which the Town could not afford on its
own.
As a member of the Massachusetts Municipal Association (MMA), Lunenburg benefits from special services
such as VACS, a computerized parking ticket processing service for the collection of parking fines which now
come directly to the Town. Effective January 1, 1983, Lunenburg became a member of the MMA organized Mass-
achusetts Interlocal Insurance Association, which pools several municipalities for Workers Compensation In-
surance at considerable savings to the taxpayers.
4
===PAGE 7===
MMA represents the interests of local government on Beacon Hill through the initiation of legislation re-
quested by the membership, lobbying activity on proposals advanced by others, and as an intervener with state
regulatory agencies on proposals which would adversely impact municipalities. Examples of their successes
are the transfer of power for the control of County budgets from the Legislature to County Advisory Boards which
are made up of representatives from each city and town in the County; effective lobbying for increased local aid
and the implementation of Proposition 2½; and modification of proposed ambulance regulations which would
have imposed unnecessary costs to municipalities.
Less tangible, but equally important, are the many benefits derived from Town officials' participation in
state and regional organizations. Your Selectmen, Town Counsel, Police Chief, Fire Chief, Highway Superinten-
dent, Treasurer, and Finance Committee are only some of those actively involved in such organizations. These
people become a voice for Lunenburg and an influence beyond our Town lines; and in learning from the experi-
ence of others and by gathering information and ideas, they are better able to serve you at the local level.
Lunenburg seems to have undergone something like the changing of the guard in 1982. The retirement of
Police Chief Frederick B. Hobbs after 35 years with the Department, 13 years as Chief, was to be only the first.
In December, Fire Chief James P. Deming after 39 years, 19 as Chief, also retired. Their many years of service
speak well for the stability of their Departments and for them. We wish them well in their well earned years of
retirement.
The Board of Selectmen feel especially fortunate
to have had available for promotion within their re-
spective departments two men of outstanding ability
and dedication. Sergeant Hector J. Morin, Jr. was
named Chief of Police in September. In January as
this report was being written, Lieutenant Dennis M.
Carrier was named Fire Chief.
The resounding victory of Mary L. Padula to the
State Senate in November heralds a change in the
Town Hall in three elected positions held by her for
30 years. We do not know who the voters will choose
as Town Clerk, Tax Collector, or Treasurer, or even
whether it will be one person or three, but we do know
that Mary leaves some very big shoes to fill. We wish
Senator Padula the very best in her mission to bring
the voice of local government and this area to the
State House, and we promise to write and call often!
Finally, we salute all those individuals who labor
on behalf of Lunenburg. Whether elected or appointed,
employees or volunteers, you are the spirit of Town
Government. You will be the guides through whatever
changes the future holds, and in you, Lunenburg
puts its trust - with thanks.
Timothy S. Hillman, Town Counsel

Board of Health
The following report was received by this Board from J. David Naparstek, Director of Public Health, of the
Nashoba Associated Boards of Health which consists of fifteen member towns.
Boards of Health are among the oldest town boards in Massachusetts. Your Board of Health is responsible
for the preservation and promotion of the health of the Town's residents. Certain police powers have been dele-
gated to them by the Commonwealth, such as the enforcement of the Sanitary and Environmental Codes and the
control of communicable diseases. Other responsibilities relate to promoting health in the community in such
areas as clinics, dental health and health education.
The Nashoba Associated Boards of Health helps your Board of Health carry out both the mandated and the
recommended public health activities. Your Board in association with other member Boards, decides what services
Nashoba will offer; Nashoba's professional staff provides these services.
Nashoba Nursing Service - The Nashoba Nursing Service offers public health and/or home health care ser-
vices for Nashoba's member towns. The following services were provided in 1982:

On April 14, 1982, a major fire struck the Nashoba Health Center in Ayer. As the result of that fire, some
statistical reports of activities were lost. An attempt is made, in this report, to reconstruct the number and type
of services provided as accurately as possible. This fire incapacitated Nashoba's environmental laboratory for
four months.

Planning Board
H
!!!!!!!!!
IIIIII
Planning Board - seated left to right - James A. Mann, Charles H. B. Arning and Chairman Robert J. Ebersole. Not
present when photo was taken were Patricia E. Lewis and James M. Sudolnik.
As a result of action taken at the 1982 Annual Town Meeting, the Planning Board has been intensely involved
in the updating of the Master Plan. After an appropriation was voted by the Town, the Board appointed Charles
Arning as Chairman of the Master Plan Sub-committee, and then solicited proposals from over twenty well quali-
fied consulting firms, from which six were selected for interviews. The public interviews were conducted at the
Town Hall. Anderson-Nichols was selected chiefly due to the extensive master planning experience of Burk
Ketchem, the person in charge of the project. The firm has completed an inventory of the Town, held several
meetings with the Board, and individually with department heads and private citizens. Public workshops have
been held to solicit input so that the plan will be the Town's plan and not the Planning Board's.
The Planning Board will not present any major changes in Lunenburg's operation until the 1984 Annual Town
Meeting to allow for further input on the proposed Master Plan update. This updating is important because of the
recommendations that result and the process itself. The process causes more communication than normal be-
tween town citizens, boards and employees. It further allows citizens to express their desire of where Lunenburg
should be in the future. The cooperation of all involved in the process is greatly appreciated by this Board.
At the 1982 Annual Town Meeting the Board presented, and the Meeting voted, a comprehensive Flood Plain
mapping and zoning provision. The Federal Government mandated that we adopt a regulation to deal with building
within flood plains. The 100-year flood plain areas are subject to the requirement of special permits for any building,
filling or excavating. This will allow the Planning Board to assure the protection of public health, safety, general
welfare and property and life, as well as preserve the natural flood control and storage characteristics, ground
water table and water recharge areas.
The driveway regulations in the Zoning By-law were revised to close a loophole that existed in the section
adopted in 1981. Driveways are now required to be located through the frontage and access strip of the lot on
which the building is located. This will help prevent those trying to circumvent the Subdivision Control Law. It was
voted to pass over the tractor-trailer regulation article due to the inclusion of vehicles not intended to be covered,
as well as to provide time for clarifying the language of the regulation. This problem should be addressed after the
Master Plan is adopted.
8
===PAGE 11===
Subdivisions were on the agenda of the Board again this year. Canterbury Drive, off Arbor Street, has seen
the underground utilities, drainage and rough grading installed. The Board has inspected the site and has allowed
the developer to make minor changes to adapt to the topography and natural growth of the area. This subdivision
has 11 lots and is expected to be completed in 1983. The preliminary subdivision submitted May 1981 for the end
of Island Road is no longer effective as no definitive plan was submitted within the statutory time. If the owners
desire to develop the property, they will have to submit a new plan and comply with the current regulations.
The Board has kept informed regarding the Sewerage and Drainage Investigative Committee, and has re-
quested information relative to the effect on growth due to any sewerage project. The necessary services must
be provided, but the Town needs to know what ramifications there might be.
Chairman Ebersole attended a conference of the Massachusetts Federation of Planning Boards held in
November. He has noted that there has been an increase in calls received for rulings on the buildability of lots
in town.
The Board has approached other towns in the area in an attempt to provide channels of communication
between the towns. Lunenburg is in a position in which it is affected more by what other towns and cities do than
what is done in the Town.
The Board would like to express its appreciation for the cooperation received from other Town Boards and
Town Citizens.
Board of Assessors
Board of Assessors - left to right - Secretary Jacqueline A. Keeler, William F. Reed, Chairman Bernard J. Paton
and Kathryn M. Herrick.
STATE MANDATED REVALUATION AND HOW LUNENBURG COMPLIED WITH THE LAW.
In 1978 the State Supreme Court ruled that all cities and towns must value all taxable property at 100% full
and fair of market value, (the Sudbury Decision) and that this mandate must be completed no later than Fiscal
Tax year 1983.
The State Department of Local Assessment came to Lunenburg in 1981 and checked our real estate sales
for the last three years and found that our property was valued between 40% to 50% of full market value. They
also noted that even though Lunenburg had not had a revaluation for twelve years, our property values were well
maintained.
9
===PAGE 12===
We were then notified by the State Department of Corporation & Taxation that Lunenburg would have to
have a complete revaluation of all Personal, Commercial and Residential property and that this revaluation, when
completed, would have to be certified by the State. All values must be between 90 and 110% in order to receive
certification, and Lunenburg would not be able to send out their Fiscal 1983 tax bills until this certification was
approved.
The Board of Assessors met with Jane Malme, Commissioner, to discuss ways that Lunenburg could comply
with the 100% full value law and after much deliberation the Board of Assessors voted to put two articles in the
1981 Annual Town Meeting Warrant for the purpose of a complete revaluation. The first article would have the
Assessors value the Town with professional assistance for Commercial and Personal property and the second
article called for a complete revaluation by a professional firm. The voters approved the second article.
The Town received three bids by Revaluation companies and your Assessors selected the firm of Finnegan
Associates. The work was started in October 1981 and after eighteen months and several meetings with the
Bureau of Local Assessment, complying with their many demands, the revaluation was completed. The State
Department of Corporation and Taxation certified that the new values were at 106% of full market value on De-
cember 15, 1982.
On December 20, 1982 the Town Clerk's office mailed out the new tax bills. Last year's tax rate was $44.00
per thousand and this year's tax rate is $19.40 per thousand.
Your Assessors would like to thank the Townspeople for their cooperation shown throughout the revaluation.
The Board of Assessors wishes to remind taxpayers of certain exemptions that have been enacted concern-
ing the value of property, both residential and non-residential. Some of these exemptions are subject to qualifi-
cations of the land itself, while others are dependent upon qualifications of the owners. These exemptions may
not exceed specific amounts. If a taxpayer feels that he qualifies for any of the following exemptions, he should
contact the Assessors' Office and request an application which lists all the requirements that must be met.
Section 17C
-Widows, minors whose fathers are deceased, and persons over 70 - $2,000 in valuation
or a tax of $175, whichever is greater.
Section 22
-Disabled or wounded veterans, or their wives, widows or parents - $2,000 to $10,000
valuation or a tax from $175 to $875, whichever is greater depending on degree of disability.
Section 22E
-Veterans with yearly certificate from Veterans' Administration indicating 100% disability.
Section 37
-Blind persons - $5,000 valuation or a tax of $437.50, whichever is greater.
Section 41
-Persons aged 70 or over - $4,000 valuation or a tax of $500, whichever is greater.
Section 41A
-Tax deferral for certain persons 65 years of age or over with gross receipts of under $20,000
annually to place the taxes on his domicile and one acre of land on a deferred payment
plan, the taxes to be paid upon settlement of the estate plus 8% interest.
Section 42
-Unmarried widows of police officers or firefighters killed in the line of duty - $8,000 valu-
ation or a tax of $700, whichever is greater.
Section 43
-Minor orphans of police officers or firefighters killed in the line of duty - $8,000 valuation
or a tax of $700, whichever is greater.
Clause 61A
Section 9
-Provides for assessment of agricultural or horticultural land at value based upon its agri-
cultural or horticultural use.
Clause 61
Section 1-6
-Provides for assessment of forest land managed under a planned program and certifi-
cation of a State Forester.
Chapter 61B
Recreational Land
-(1) Provides for assessment of land not less than five acres if it is retained in substantially
a natural, wild or open condition or in a landscaped condition in such a manner as to
allow to a significant extent the preservation of wildlife and other natural resources. (2)
Land deemed to be recreational land which is devoted primarily to recreational use and is
available to the general public or members of a non-profit organization.

Cemetery Commission
Cemetery Commission: Seated M Chairman Caroline
New Well House in the South Cemetery.
C. Porter and George D. Reichard. Standing - G.
Barry Whitcomb and Superintendent David L. Porter.
This past year, the Cemetery Commissioners have been busy overseeing the workings of the Cemetery
Department and keeping within the confines of Proposition 2½. Several meetings have been held and discussions
have taken place relative to the feasibility of utilizing different land areas within the Cemetery.
The Cemetery Department has tried to keep its machinery and buildings in good repair and have held off
making large capital expenses. Our Superintendent has done a good job maintaining the fertilization, mowing,
tree removal and maintenance programs within both cemeteries.
We wish to thank Mr. and Mrs. William R. Tapply and family for their donation of a new well house in the
South Cemetery. All who use this new well house will certainly appreciate their generosity.

Park Commission
The year 1982 was a year of change for the Park Commission. Three new Commissioners were elected,
Martha M. Johnson, Robert A. Membrino and Joseph A. Giovino, Chairman. We wish to thank our predecessors
Richard A. Kinney, Richard J. Bishop and Armand E. St. Arnaud for their guidance and express gratitude on behalf
of the Town for their excellent performance over the years.
Proposition 2½ was the dominant force in our budgeting and the services provided to the Town. Salaries
and fixed costs increased, which necessitated cutbacks in services provided to the residents. This situation is
continuing into 1983 and to compensate, user fees and/or a reduction in services must be implemented. Most of
our facilities are in need of repair. The budgetary process has and is neglecting the capital assets of our com-
munity, which, in the not too distant future, will mean the loss of these to the residents. In an attempt to preserve
the facilities and eliminate hazards several articles will be placed on the Town Warrant.
A review of the programs offered: the Town Beach, under the auspices of Jerry Beloin, saw a decrease in
usage due to the mild summer and the increase in fees implemented several years ago. However, it still remains
as one of the most widely used facilities by Town residents. The Summer Recreational Program, headed by Dick
Stachowicz was again a model for the area. Fitchburg again won the championship of the eight-team High School
Basketball League. The Men's Softball playoff championship was won by the Lakeview Club. All Star Sporting
Goods won the divisional championship. Six teams participated in the League. The Arts & Crafts Program, Wallis
Playground, Center Playground and the Junior Basketball Program provided the Town youth with a needed recre-
ational program during the summer months. Day trips and picnics to Benson's Animal Farm, Canobie Lake Park,
Willard Brook and Salisbury Beach were well attended. Our thanks to the Southside Activities Club for donating
the use of their facilities for the children this past summer. The Junior Track Meet at Marshall Park highlighted
the summer program. This winter the Biddy League Basketball, Teen League, Men's Program and open gym
sponsored by the Park Department enjoyed its best year with attendance and usage substantially higher. A Girls'
Basketball team was entered into the Leominster League for the first time. It was coached by Sandy Flanagan.
Ron Lamothe coached the seventh and eighth grade team consisting of players from the Biddy League Basketball
playing other schools and towns. It was highly successful and beneficial for the players.
Our Park Superintendent, Greg Bingham, and his assistant, Randy Boutwell, did yeoman work in keeping our
parks in excellent condition. Preparation and coordination of the facilities for high school baseball, track, girls'
and men's softball, Little League and soccer were of the highest standards. Many nights there were over eight
games held simultaneously from Farm League Ball to Men's Softball. Our park facilities continue to provide for
picnic and recreational outings for many local groups. We wish to thank the Little League for the construction of
a refreshment stand at the Brian J. McNally Park (formerly Lesure Field). The Park Commissioners implemented
a name change to recognize Brian's courage and spirit in order to provide a model for the youth of our community
to identify with. A special thanks to all those who have participated in our programs and made them a success.
13
===PAGE 16===
Housing Authority
The Lunenburg Housing Authority is in its thirteenth year in the operation of the facilities at 131 White Street
known as the Pearl Brook Housing for the Elderly. Presently, we have forty-three apartments occupied by single
persons and five occupied by couples.
The tenants have organized the Pearl Brook Tenants' Association which meets on a regular basis and ar-
ranges interesting projects for the benefit and enjoyment of its members. Mr. Eugene P. DesLauriers holds the
office of President. He is the principal liaison between the tenants and the Authority members.
The tenants are provided with weekly transportation. The shut-ins are provided with "Meals-on-Wheels' and
the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health provide the Well-Oldster Clinics monthly for the tenants. Home Health
Care provides household duties and shopping.
The requirements for occupancy are that the applicants be at least 65 years of age and in need of good
housing. Rents are variable in that no more than 25% of Income can be charged for rent which includes heat,
water, and electricity. The rising cost of energy makes it very difficult for the Authority to keep within their budget.
We invite anyone who is interested in our facilities to inquire at the Housing Authority Office where Mrs.
Eileen Alleva, Executive Secretary, will be available to answer questions. You may call 345-7655 or 342-5308.
Housing Authority - left to right - Executive Secretary Elleen M. Alleva, Lance D. May, Josephine C.
Meloon, Richard I. White and Judith K. Tarbell. Not present when photo was taken was Chairman Ella
Rae Denault.

===PAGE 17===
Library Trustees
Library Trustees - Seated left to right - Barbara T. Murray, Kathleen M. Pliska, June Dettenrieder and
Director Elizabeth H. Burnap. Standing left to right - Edward A. O'Brien and Chairman Patrick J. Slattery.
Not present when photo was taken was Sheila A. Jalutkewicz.
How do the trustees and staff of a library know when their department has had a successful year? This is a
question that has the local library people wondering. Ordinarily, a library is said to be succeeding when it attracts
increasing numbers of people, offers more services, and circulates more materials than in the previous year.
Measured by these standards, Ritter Memorial Library has been progressing in the right direction for several
years.
Circulation of all materials has increased 29.5% in the past three years. In the first five months of FY83,
circulation has increased another 5% over the same time period last year. Due to demand, the pre-school story
hours have been increased from one to two every week, and enrollment in the children's summer reading program
in 1982 was the largest ever. It could be said that the library is experiencing the direct opposite of declining en-
rollment.
15
===PAGE 18===
In the same period of time, the cost of all library services has gone from $7.61 per capita to $8.64 per capita,
a rise of only 13.5% without even mentioning the effect of inflation on purchasing power. When the revenue from
library fines and state aid (granted because the library meets specific standards for personnel, hours open, and
amount expended on books) is deducted from the budget, the cost per capita in FY82 was only $7.70, much less
than the average price of one book.
Keeping expenses down, as required by Proposition 2½, was-accomplished by reducing staff, cutting open
hours, buying fewer books, economizing on supplies, and keeping the building very cool in winter. More success
the library has succeeded in living with 2½! But the dual successes of staying within the budget and increasing
services cannot add up to a successful operation indefinitely. A 29.5% increase in circulation means a 29.5%
increase in the amount of work to be done checking materials out and back in, and an increase in wear and tear
on all library materials and property.
One reason that the library has been able to cope as well as it has is the continuing outstanding support by
the Friends of the Library. Their fund-raising projects included a yard sale, a book sale, two roller skating parties,
a boutique table, and rental books. They sponsored the annual Children's Art Festival and "Third Thursday", a
monthly series for adults featuring International Nights, movies, and speakers. With the School Department, they
co-sponsored a visit from children's author, Lois Lowry, who spoke informally at the Middle School and then met
with interested youngsters at Ritter Memorial Library. The Friends gave volunteer assistance at various library
functions and provided refreshments and materials for numerous programs. They paid for all performers for the
summer reading program events, as well as awards at the end of the program. The appearance of the upper level
of the building has been greatly improved by the Friends' gift of end panels for the book shelves. Passes to the
Fine Arts, DeCordova, and Children's museums, and the New England Aquarium have been purchased by the
Friends and can be borrowed from the library for 24 hours by any adult patron.
Children's services, under. the direction of Emily Foster, continued to be a major part of the library operation.
In addition to the four pre-school story hours, there are story hours for Bon Jour Nursery School and for each
Primary School class. Following the great success of "Lois Lowry Day", plans are underway for a similar event
next spring with another author and the help of an award of $200 already received from the Massachusetts Arts
Lottery. The very popular summer reading program, with the theme "Treasures for the Taking", attracted 145
children who read 3,910 books. Summer activities included: a clown show, movies, puppet show, a chalk-drawing
contest, a magician, and a treasure hunt. Throughout the year, craft workshops and movies for various age groups
rounded out a complete schedule of children's services.
The Trustees sponsored a logo-design contest with the winning entry being submitted by Betsy Rivers. The
new logo will appear on bookmarks, stationery, and other items in the library, and is featured on the new sign
being placed at the corner of Massachusetts Avenue and Leominster Road. Painting of the sign has been donated
by Ken-Bon Sign Service, and the planting around it was made possible by a substantial gift from the Lunenburg
Memorial Fund. Other gifts received this year included: a subscription to "Fine Woodworking" from the Lunenburg
Center for the Arts and Crafts in memory of Vincent Loiselle, donations of money from the Pearl Brook Association
and the Montachusett Area Newcomer's Club, and new and used books from persons too numerous to list.
The burgeoning circulation figures include: 24,293 items borrowed from the adult collection; 27,157 from
the children's collection; and 1,789 from the Young Adult section, for a total circulation of 53,239. In addition to
books, materials borrowed included magazines, records, 16mm films and projector, pamphlets, museum passes,
and children's filmstrips and puzzles. Many other materials and services were used in the library --- newspapers,
telephone directories, college catalogs, high school yearbooks, maps, and of particular importance, the photo-
copier.
Before the end of FY81, when circulation had increased almost 8,000 items in a single year, the need for
more staff hours was obvious, but with the passage of Proposition 2½, it has been necessary to manage with
even less help. Townspeople must realize that this situation is reaching a critical point. If the library that they have
supported for so many years is to continue to provide the services that the Town expects, the means must be
found to put more staff hours in the budget. The only alternative seems to be deliberately to discourage use of the
library, and the staff and Trustees really can't picture themselves planning to do that!
16
===PAGE 19===
Fire Department
During 1982, the number of calls for assistance
received by the Fire Department was 436. This was
down 13% from 1981 and down 16% from 1980,
principally due to a reduced number of Rescue
Calls. The calls were as follows: Structure - 67;
Vehicle - 24; Brush - 18; Rubbish - 3; Accident
Standby - 7; Wash Down - 7; Power Lines - 4; LP
Gas Tank - 1; Water Rescue - 3; Mutual Aid - 23;
Needless/False - 21; Service Calls - 15; Medical
(no rescue) - 3; Rescue/Ambulance - 241. Included
in the number of structure fire alarms were 28
Chimney Fires. The number of chimney fires con-
tinues to increase yearly as a direct result of the
number of people heating with wood. Homeowners
are reminded to clean their chimneys periodically
and to have proper, code complying stove and
furnace installations, along with appropriate smoke
detector maintenance.
The group call system implemented in 1981 to
cope with Proposition 2½ continues to work well.
This has resulted in lower payroll costs for calls
that can be handled with fewer firefighters. The
risk associated with this system is that there is a
chance of a reduced response of men and equip-
ment to fires that should have a full response. Full
alarms for reported building fires have had an
average of 18 firefighters responding with fires at
night having a higher number of men.
James P. Deming, Fire Chief
Our most significant equipment addition during
(Retired December 24, 1982)
1982 was the placement in service of 3,000 feet of
large diameter, water supply hose. This has greatly
enhanced our ability to provide an adequate water supply over long distances to the scene of a fire.
In 1982, 1,143 man-hours were spent by the members of the Fire Department for firefighting training. Only
one-third of this time was covered by the payroll, resulting in over 760 man-hours for training being donated by
the officers and firefighters.
Recertification training by the Department's Emergency Medical Technicians is not included in the fire
training amounts. This medical training requires about forty hours per EMT per year and even more for our Ad-
vanced EMT's. This results in an additional 1,200 man-hours of medical training per year. Each firefighter as a
minimum requirement continues to be certified in CPR and Red Cross Advanced First Aid.
Also during 1982, the Town was surveyed by the Insurance Services Office (ISO) in order to establish a new
classification for insurance rating purposes. Due to significant improvements of the Fire Department over the
last several years, and the available water supply for firefighting, a substantially better rating should be obtained
and will become effective during the Spring of 1983. This will result in lower fire insurance rates for most building
owners within the Town.
For the third year, we are postponing replacement of our 1961 pumper. This truck still runs well but its re-
liability due to corrosion, breakdowns, and parts availability is very low. During 1982, while pumping at a fire,
this truck suffered a complete brake failure. Fortunately, proper safety precautions had been taken and there
were no negative consequences but the results would have been different if the failure had occurred while the
17
===PAGE 20===
truck was responding to the incident. In addition, even with the present economy, fire apparatus costs have
steadily increased at 15-20% per year. The expected replacement cost for this truck in Fiscal 1985 will be about
$130,000.
The year 1983 will bring a change in leadership to the Department. Fire Chief James P. Deming, a member of
the Fire Department for 39 years and Fire Chief for nearly 19 years, retired in December. Jim's dedication to the
Town and to the members of the Fire Department will be missed, and we wish him well in his retirement.
Dennis M. Carrier, a 22 year veteran of the Department will replace Chief Deming. Chief Carrier would like
to thank the members of the Fire Department for their continued cooperation during 1982, and he looks forward
to working with them in the future.

Highway Department
Andy Rooney, columnist, humorist and com-
menter on TV's "60 Minutes" has carved a suc-
cessful career by discussing mundane things in an
entertaining manner. While shuffling collections of
cereal boxes, keys, or toothpaste tubes, he stares
earnestly at us from the TV screen while he com-
ments humorously about the problems we all en-
counter with familiar objects.
Because he has captured the interest of SO
many people, public works professionals found a
recent column of his, "The Worries I Can Handle 11
of special interest. In his own inimitable way, he
wrote about the worries political leaders face, such
as the $41 billion bill for fixing our bridges, the cost
of making all public facilities available to handi-
capped people, reconstructing entire sewer sys-
tems and preventing hazardous wastes from fouling
our water supply.
His conclusion is that we know how to solve
these tremendous, expensive problems, but we're
not always willing to sacrifice today's pleasures
for tomorrow's needs. He admits that he can't
handle such problems himself, but that mayors,
governors and other officials can't continue to
dodge these issues. His own problems, he says,
relate more to whether the car will start, whether
there's enough hot water for a shower, and whether
there's enough coffee for breakfast. We owe Andy
Rooney our gratitude. He's telling our story, and
he's telling it to a much wider audience than we'll
George G. Munyon, Jr., Highway Superintendent
ever command.
Time Magazine recently emphasized that public works budget-cutting began not with President Reagan but
nearly two decades ago. In 1965, 3.6% of the United States' gross national product was being invested in public
works facilities. This percentage has been steadily declining since that date and now stands at 1.7%. This con-
stitutes a reduction of 52.8%. Is it any wonder, then, that our roads, bridges, sewerage systems, water facilities
and public buildings are wearing out?
The Council of State Planning Agencies warns that: "The deteriorated conditions of basic facilities that
underpin the economy will prove a critical bottleneck to national economic renewal during this decade unless
we can find ways to finance public works."
(Excerpt from Editorial, American Public Works Association.)
Due to the effect of Proposition 2½, our budgets were cut and articles eliminated. We had to terminate a
good man, cut out all our former high school or college, summer help, curtail the promptness of some of our
services and shift personnel around to complete priority projects, thus retarding certain work such as patching,
roadside mowing plus the promptness of attending to requests by Townspeople.
Labor - $166,352.22 was spent performing all aspects of highway related work.
Chapter 90 - The intersection of Electric Avenue and Whalom Road was redesigned and repaved which greatly
improved this dangerous area. A mix-in-place surface job was completed on a section of Lancaster Avenue.
Drainage work was performed on Chase Road. A total of $58,640.00 was spent under this account.
19
===PAGE 22===
General Highway Maintenance - Under the Road Oil & Tar category of this account, oiling was done on Reservoir,
Mulpus, Cross Road, Sunnyhill, Beal, New West Townsend, West, Pleasant, Pratt, Wallis Park, Florence, Youngs,
Weatherbee, Baker, Kingman, East, Burke, Pearl Brook, West Acres, Broadmeadow, Page Hill, Peninsula Drive
and Island Road at a cost of $51,247.64. Approximately 449 tons of patch was applied to roads all over Town at
a cost of $11,791.26. This account also encompasses catch basin cleaning, roadside mowing, sweeping; drainage
work and brush cutting. The total spent under this account was $112,843.70.
Snow Removal - Last winter was the most severe since 1978. A total of $82,098.56 was spent in order to keep
our roads free from snow and ice.
Snow & Ice Removal, Private Roads - $6,139.01 was spent under this account.
Road Machinery Maintenance - All maintenance and materials required, plus the purchase of gas, oil, grease,
diesel fuel, acetylene, oxygen, plow and grader blades, chains and small tools come under this account. A total
of $71,306.43 was spent to maintain the Town's equipment in top condition.
Town Barn Maintenance - Heat, light, telephone, janitorial and maintenance supplies, stationery supplies and
postage, office equipment and repairs, special building maintenance and Superintendent's expense account cost
$10,463.85. In an effort to keep heating costs down, two heating units were turned off and one unit kept at 50
degrees.
Special Accounts - Drainage was installed in Holman, Natick, Page, Charlton, Chestnut, Northfield, New West
Townsend, White, Hollis and West Streets at a cost of $16,137.98. The excavation and materials for the recon-
struction of Highland Street cost $7,169.04. The purchase and fabrication of street signs cost $852.48. The
purchase of a street sign machine cost $3,680.00.
Police Department
The year 1982 brought many changes to the
Lunenburg Police Department. A new Police Chief,
Hector J. Morin, Jr., was appointed September 27,
1982 to fill the vacancy created by the retirement
of former Chief Frederick B. Hobbs. Chief Hobbs
joined the Lunenburg Police Department in the
summer of 1947. He was appointed Sergeant in
April of 1955. He was appointed Acting Chief of
Police in October 1968 and full-time Chief of Police
on January 27, 1969. On June 4, 1982, a retirement
party was given for "Fred" at the Bull Run Resta-
urant and the building was filled to capacity with
more than 500 people turning out to pay tribute to
this man who served the Town of Lunenburg faith-
fully for the last 35 years. The best of luck in your
retirement, "Fred".
Sergeant Lambert was moved from the night shift
to the day shift serving as the court officer for the
Town of Lunenburg to fill the vacancy by the pro-
motion of Sergeant Morin to Chief of Police - leaving
a night Sergeant's position vacant; hopefully, to be
filled the first of the year. Patrolman John E. Baker
is still on injury leave convalescing from a leg injury
he received from a hit and run motorist on October
Frederick B. Hobbs, Retired Police Chief
1, 1979. Ms. Debra F. DiPadua was appointed as

===PAGE 23===
Lunenburg Police Department - Seated left to right - Patrolmen John E. Baker, George E. Aho, James
P. Marino, Leo J. Janssens, II and Stanley W. Barney. Standing left to right - Patrolmen Alphonse J.

a regular member of the Lunenburg Police Department on May 2, 1982 to fill the vacancy left by the resignation of
Patrolman John A. Busack on July 14, 1981. Ms. DiPadua resigned on July 14, 1982 and accepted an appointment
to the Massachusetts State Police Academy. Mr. James P. Marino was appointed to the Police Department on
September 17, 1982 to fill the vacancy created by Ms. DiPadua's resignation. Patrolman Marino successfully
completed a 12 week basic training course held at the Worcester Police Academy and graduated on December
10, 1982. He is presently working the Midnight to 8AM shift.
In October, a Firearms Qualification course was given to all full-time officers and reserve officers of the
Lunenburg Police Department. The course was conducted by Patrolman Steven G. Whitney and Patrolman John
E. Baker, both qualified firearms instructors, with all members of the full-time and the reserve department suc-
cessfully qualifying. I would like to acknowledge our appreciation to the Police Relief Association for donating
3,700 rounds of ammunition for the Firearms Training and to Steve and John for donating their time and expertise
to make this endeavor a success. I would also like to take this opportunity to acknowledge the regular and part-
time police officers who have taken courses and training at their own time and expense. Regular officers - Patrol-
men George E. Aho, Stanley W. Barney, Alphonse J. Baron, Daniel F. Bourgeois, Leo J. Janssens, II, and Steven
G. Whitney for courses in Tear Gas Training, Alcohol Enforcement, Street Survival, Criminal Law Update and
Night Combat Shooting. Reserve officers - John M. Alwood, Charles T. Billiter, John A. Busack, Charles Deming,
Jr., Ernest Gould, III, Paul G. Grunditz, John G. Massack, Matthew J. T. Ryan and Leon C. Tarbell for courses in
Tear Gas Training, Night Combat Shooting, Motor Vehicle Law, Radar Training, Revolver Familiarization, Inter-
view and Interrogation, Breathalyzer Training and Rape and Alcohol Enforcement. Lunenburg should be proud to
have these dedicated individuals who are willing to give of their own time and money to better serve the com-
munity and increase their knowledge as police officers.
As your newly appointed Police Chief, I will strive to increase the effectiveness and efficiency of the police
services in the community, keeping in mind that the basic purpose of the Police Department is the enforcement
of laws, preventing and detecting crime and maintaining order within the community. The best way to accomp-
lish this is to do a crime study analysis of the Town and of those crimes that are the most serious, stimulate the
greatest fear and cause the greatest economic losses. These should be afforded the highest priorities when it
comes to the spending of Police resources.
There were 7,118 miscellaneous calls made to the Police Department during 1982. This is an average of
better than 593 calls per month. There were 303 accidents that required service and two of these were fatalities.
The total motor vehicle violations, including routine stops, was 2,017. The Department took into protective custody
37 persons during 1982 and answered 635 alarms, false or otherwise. There were 258 various firearm permits
issued, 240 summons served and 521 house checks made. The tabulation which follows is an accounting for the
court cases handled by the Police Department in 1982.

Veterans' Agent
The operation of this Department has greatly
improved over the past few years due largely to
the State's installation of a new computer system.
This new system prevents the fraudulent wasting
of funds and insures that all recipients are handled
in a more efficient manner.
This year your Veterans' Agent was able to
remain within the appropriations of this Depart-
ment, and has also continued to recover funds from
delinquent veterans. With unemployment on the
rise, the number of recipients has increased. There
are at present six active cases on the rolls.
I will be retiring on June 30, 1983 for health
reasons. At this time I want to give a very special
thank you to the wonderful people of Lunenburg
for the courtesy, hospitality and cooperation rendered
to me for many years. I also wish to thank the Town
Alfred H. Gauthier, Veterans' Agent
Clerk and her staff for the assistance they have
given me. I will miss my many friends in the Town
Hall. I leave with the happy and fulfilling feeling that I have helped my fellow veterans in times of need.

===PAGE 25===
Personnel Committee
Personnel Committee - left to right - Anne M. Davis, Chairman John L. Plank, Jr., George Thomas and Lynn F.
Markham. Not present when photo was taken was Beatrice E. Stepton.
In spite of Proposition 2½, the Town has experienced little in the way of service cuts due to the loyalty of our
employees, even though their numbers have been thinned by budget related layoffs.
After conducting many meetings and obtaining as much input as possible from various boards, committees
and the Town employees, the Personnel Committee has decided on the Salary and Wage Classification Schedule
as presented in the Finance Committee Report and proposed for adoption at the 1983 Annual Town Meeting. The
Personnel Committee is acutely aware of the severe financial problems faced by the Town at this time, and realizes
that the proposed Schedule represents a slightly greater increase than strictest attention to budgetary constraints
would dictate; but also feels that it represents the minimum increase which would be fair and consistent with the
absolute needs of the Town employees.
Reclassification of jobs has been minimal. The mileage rate is proposed to move to $.20 per mile.
The Personnel Committee hopes the Town will agree to all facets of the Plan as proposed with the clear un-
derstanding that inequities which now exist must be leveled out in ensuing years.

===PAGE 26===
Conservation Commission
Conservation Commission - Seated left to right - Robert C. Leupold, Bessie H. Lindsay and John C.
Lahti. Standing left to right - Robert A. Garneau, Glenn S. Stillman and Chairman Raymond G. Chapde-
laine. Not present when photo was taken was Nancy I. Sholl.
It seems that every day brings a news story that tells of another disaster to some city or town water supply.
While all of us are aware that the news media blows most stories out of proportion, they do help us realize the
importance and the fragility of our water supplies.
The anguish, fear and helplessness shown by those affected by the loss of their water supply should serve
to warn us to protect our local water systems, both public and private.
The Conservation Commission supports the concept of proper zoning laws and the control of sanitary dis-
posal systems in the outlying areas to promote the protection of dug and driven wells. In the case of the public
water supply, we as citizens of the Town must work toward the protection of the watershed that feeds the aquifier
supplying our wells with some of the best water in New England.
The Commonwealth of Massachusetts through the Farm Preservation Act is in the process of buying the
development rights from several agricultural tracts in Lunenburg. We are fortunate that the tract is located in our
watershed area. This will reduce the number of homes that could be built in this area and will serve to reduce the
amount of septic tank effluent upstream of our water supply. The Conservation Commission is sponsoring an
article in the Town Warrant concerning the purchase of development rights and is seeking the support of the
Townspeople at this year's Annual Town Meeting.
24
===PAGE 27===
Council on Aging
This year has seen a vital increase in the services offered to the Seniors of Lunenburg as the hours of bus
service were more than doubled, and a limited amount of Van Service for the handicapped was inaugurated. It
was only with the strong support of the citizens of Lunenburg expressed at the Annual Town Meeting that we were
able to expand our program. During the first half of this fiscal year, our budget for this cause has been fully utilized.
Heartfelt thanks to Einard Warpula and also to Milton Carpenter and Bill Dahlgren for overseeing this effort.
The Christmas Dinner this year cost the Seniors $3.00 instead of the $2.00 previously charged. We appreci-
ated being able to use the Turkey Hill School for this festive event. We hope that more Seniors will enjoy this dinner
next year. Sincere thanks to Martha Molaghan for her guidance in this program.
Hot Meals for Seniors, who have a need for a tasty and nutritious meal, are delivered to their homes Monday
through Friday by Sandra Flanagan and her small corps of drivers. New volunteers are needed to help deliver
these meals. There is no pay, but automobile expenses are covered.
The Drop-In Center continues to flourish and is open from 10 A.M. until 1 P.M. An average of fifty Seniors
gather at the Drop-In Center which is located in the United Parish Building. This year we regretfully said "Farewell"
to "Marcy" Peed, who has been the heart of the program since its inception. We wish her well in her move to
Ohio. Her love and helpfulness are greatly missed, but other volunteers have stepped in to continue the work.
We seem to have more and more cooperation between activities of the Council and the independent group of
Seniors from Lunenburg called "The Friendly Seniors". We wish to see this trend continue. Chet Pliska, a member
of our Council, is this year's President of the Friendly Seniors and functions as a bridge of communication. We
hope to restore the Newsletter to at least a quarterly circulation, as we plan to go to a computerized mailing list
of Town Seniors.
We deeply appreciate the support of the Townspeople, and we look forward to continued cooperation during
the ensuing year.
Council on Aging - Seated left to right - Lucienne Chapdelaine, Doris M. Carrier and Helen M. White.
Standing left to right - Chester J. Pliska, Chairman John E. Sandberg, Sandra P. Flanagan and Alfred
C. Gilchrest. Not present when photo was taken were Elvira Z. May, Martha L. Molaghan, Milton C.
Carpenter and Mark G. Thatcher.

===PAGE 28===
Thomas E. Mortis, Inspector of Plumbing & Gas Fittings
Lauren W. Davies, Inspector of Weights & Measures
Inspector of Plumbing and Gas Fittings
During 1982 the Inspector of Plumbing and Gas Fittings made a total of 158 inspections including 38 for
new homes, 30 for remodeling, 6 for solar heat installations, 4 for commercial buildings, 54 gas permits for
various gas appliances, 3 for commercial buildings, 15 bottled gas installations and 8 informational calls.
Last year plumbing permits for new construction were still slow due to high interest rates and inflation, but
remodeling and replacement continued at a steady pace. The demand for gas fired hot water tanks is still notice-
able with electric rates and oil prices still out of proportion. The homeowner is being forced to convert from the
use of tankless type domestic hot water to a more economical storage tank type.
Solar and energy saving hot water tank installations are also on the rise. Any device or appliance for heating
and storing water must comply with Chapter 142 of the General Laws.
Natural gas as an energy fuel is still in demand in the area. A moratorium was placed on all industrial and
residential heating equipment. Gas for gas replacements and the piping of domestic hot water tanks is still per-
mitted, but only after authorization and log numbers issued to the gas fitters. Due to the energy emergency brought
about by the severe cold last winter, Boston Gas Company and Fitchburg Gas and Electric Light Company, our
natural gas distributors, ran into supply problems and were forced to temporarily suspend the installation of new
gas and conversion heating systems. Liquefied petroleum gas installations were also on the increase. It was used
as a supplement in some buildings when natural gas was not available.
The cooperation of the Building Inspector is greatly appreciated in our effort to see that the Town of Lunenburg
Is a safe place to live.
Inspector of Weights & Measures
This year the fees charged by this Department were slightly increased by the acceptance of Article 44 at
the 1982 Annual Town Meeting. The revenue of this Department increased by $160.00 due to the new fee schedule.
There were a total of 34 scales inspected and sealed and 1 scale adjusted. These scales range in size from
railroad scales, truck scales to small produce scales. Also a total of 34 gasoline pumps were inspected and
sealed and 8 pumps required adjustment.
The Inspector extends his appreciation to various establishments in the Town of Lunenburg for the cooper-
ation and assistance rendered.

===PAGE 29===
Inspector of Wires
In 1982 the Inspector of Wires issued 65 wiring permits and performed 119 inspections. Generally, a wiring
inspection consists of a "rough" and "finish" stage.
All wiring performed in the Town of Lunenburg must be accompanied by the proper permit which is to be
secured only by a Massachusetts licensed electrician. Full compliance with the current Massachusetts Electric
Code must be exhibited. Types of wiring which require inspection include (but are not limited to) new residences,
remodeling existing buildings, electric service changes, swimming pools, additions, garages, electric signs and
commercial/industrial properties.
The safety and welfare of the citizens and businesses of Lunenburg make wiring inspections imperative.
Wiring permits and current fee schedules may be obtained at the Town Clerk's Office.
Building Inspector
The Building Inspector's office wishes to take this opportunity to thank the residents of Lunenburg and Town
Officials for their cooperation and assistance throughout the year. The Building Inspector's office is now located
in the Townsend Town Hall, Main Street, Townsend Center. The telephone number is 597-2837 with office hours
from 9:00 A.M. to 10:00 A.M., Monday through Thursday.
With the cost of heating continually on the rise, many residents are adding insulation to their homes. This
office has information available to assist the residents in their choice of insulation, so that the maximum benefit
will be obtained from the investment. Please feel free to contact the Building Inspector for assistance.

Animal Control Officer
As Animal Control Officer and Animal Inspector
in Lunenburg, I have had a very busy and exciting
year. Nineteen hundred calls were received - some
needing my immediate attention while others were
handled by telephone. In addition to dog calls, I
received hundreds of calls on cat, racoon and skunk
problems, etc.. Also, livestock calls increased this
year where I was summoned a number of times to
rescue COWS, horses, pigs, goats and sheep that
had strayed. Due to the vast number of calls I
receive each year, the Town assigned this de-
partment with a police radio and also has pur-
chased several humane animal traps to be used
in trapping wild animals.
We had three cases in Lunenburg in which I
was assisted by the Massachusetts Society for
Prevention of Cruelty to Animals. I was also assisted
by the Department of Natural Resources in catching
a falcon that had flown into Town and became
trapped in a barn.
Stray and abandoned animals are a large
problem in Lunenburg as everywhere, and I have
been fortunate to place 95% of all animals in good
Kathleen M. Comeau, Animal Control Officer
homes. I am also striving to attain 100% licensing
of dogs in our community.
Aside from my regular "animal chores," I was able to take four courses in animal control. This schooling
has helped me carry out my duties as Dog Officer.
I would like to take the time to thank the Selectmen, Town Counsel, Town Clerk and the Town Hall staff for
their help. A special thank you to the Police Department, Fire Department and Rescue Unit for the assistance
they have given me this year. I feel very fortunate to be working with these dedicated people.
My office is open 24 hours a day, 7 days a week. There is someone on phone duty at all times. Please call
no matter how small or large the problem is, and I will try to help.

===PAGE 31===
Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District
Nineteen hundred eighty-two was a busy year for Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School, some
of the busy-ness new, some carried over from previous years.
Graduation in June saw two hundred twenty-five graduates receive diplomas while black skies and cold
breezes prevailed, but the heavy rain held off until completion of the outdoor ceremony. Six students from the
post graduate Dental Laboratory Technician program graduated in a separate ceremony which marked the closing
of the last post graduate program at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School.
One thousand eighty-five students were present on opening day of the new school year in August, 1982. The
freshman class of three hundred twenty-seven students was the second largest in the history of the school.
Due to the funding received from approved grant proposals, Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical
School is proud to be able to offer - once again - programs for the aspiring Dental Assistant and Medical Assistant.
These programs were originally offered at the post graduate level. The new programs are for high school students,
grades 9 through 12.
The school was the fortunate recipient of gifts and the loan of two new cars for the Driver Education course,
a Mercury Marquis from Mathieu Ford of Winchendon, and a Mercury Lynx from Roberts Ford of Athol. Other gifts
included: a greenhouse, 60' X 24', from Thomas Sulin of Fitchburg, half of which has been erected, and will be
used as a laboratory for the Building and Grounds program; computer equipment from Digital Equipment Corp.;
a Diesel Engine from David Bingham of New Hampshire; a Kalamazoo Band Saw from E. T. Duval & Sons, Inc.,
Leominster; and other gifts too numerous to mention but just as greatly appreciated.
The annual Fitchburg Conference (formerly called the MAVA Conference) was held in June through the joint
efforts of the Massachusetts Department of Occupational Education and Fitchburg State College Department of
Continuing Education. Attendance was high and the programs offered were of the usual outstanding quality.
Negotiations with the teachers and custodians which consumed a great deal of effort and time for over a
year are now concluded.
The long-continued plans for expansion continued through the year and, to date, Orange, Petersham and
Westminster have voted to join the District. All eight towns and the City of Gardner have voted to accept the three
towns into the District leaving only Fitchburg uncommitted officially.
Once again, as for seven years, the students and staff joined efforts at Christmas time to raise money for a
local charity. The largest gift to date was raised in 1982, $728, and it was given to the Lunenburg Community
Council.
The gratitude of students, staff, and the District Committee is extended to all of the cities and towns of the
Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District for their continued support of the standards and
goals of the school, support which has made possible the delivery of high quality vocational education to those
hundreds of young men and women of the District who have selected this educational route into their futures.

Tree Warden
It was apparent during this year that the de-
vastation created by the invasion of caterpillars left
many trees dead, even many pine trees. However,
it is encouraging that the prediction of a three-year
cycle promises some relief for 1983.
During Fiscal 1982, 183 diseased trees were
cut, chipped and burned. The Department of Natural
Resources gave five days ($700.00) of tree removal
work and two days of tagging trees suspected of
having Dutch Elm disease. A total of $9,088.99 was
spent under the Dutch Elm Account.
Work accomplished under the supervision of
the Tree Warden amounted to $6,779.98. The Chip-
ping Program was continued whereby Townspeople
could bring brush to be chipped to the Town High-
way Garage on the second and fourth Saturdays of
the month for as long as money remained in the
account for this purpose.
Richard W. Brown, Tree Warden

Ad Hoc Recycling Committee
The Sanitary Landfill opened its recycling center on June 5, 1982. The Committee has been pleased with the
response of the Town residents who have taken the time to use the recycling center. Because of your cooperation,
we have been able to recycle the following volume of materials: 60 truckloads of bulky metal waste; 30 pick-up
truckloads of newspapers; 30 pick-up truckloads of glass; and 150 gallons of motor oil.
This corresponds to about 2% of the annual refuse. Please continue to make the recycling program success-
ful by using the recycling center for these items: bulky metal waste (large metal items such as refrigerators,
ovens, etc), newspapers, glass (clear, brown and green), aluminum cans and motor oil.
Ad Hoc Recycling Committee: left to right - David D. Blake, Chairman James Patacchiola and Oper-
ations Manager George G. Munyon, Jr. Not present when photo was taken were Paul F. Bregoli and
Nancy I. Sholl.
Town Forest Committee
The Committee has studied the feasibility of implementing a forest management plan on two of the Town
Forest plots, one off Gilchrest Street, and one off Howard Street.
The Gilchrest Street forest plot has been chosen as the site for a forest management survey. This will be
completed around March 1983. A long term plan with 5 or 10 year segments will be implemented once the
survey is complete.
A professional forester, Robert Leupold, was chosen to conduct the survey. The objective of the plan is to
improve the forest stand and generate revenue for the Town.
Town Forest Committee: left to right - Armas S. J. Heino, Chairman Scott W. Foster and Robert A. Garneau.

Regional School District Study Committee
Article 23 of the 1982 Annual Town Meeting established the creation of a five member Regional School
District Study Committee whose function is to study the advisability of forming a Regional School District with
the abutting Town of Shirley and/or other towns in the area. Such study is to include all direct and indirect aspects
of quality of education, including financial and social.
Since the formal organization of the Committee by the Board of Selectmen on June 20, 1982, we have been
meeting weekly, with members being generally responsible for input on each of the specific areas of study in
progress. As the question of regionalization has bearing on many diverse facets of town planning, school man-
agement and enrollment projection, money management and legislation, the Committee has sought to obtain a
body of information from groups and individuals representing a broad spectrum of expertise. Accordingly, we
have met in Committee with members of the State Department of Education, Lunenburg's Superintendent of
Schools, the Principal of each School, the Lunenburg Planning Board, and the Shirley Regionalization Committee.
Members of the Committee have individually undertaken assignments, compiling information and reporting
back to the Committee. Among these assignments was a meeting with an official in Charlton, Massachusetts con-
cerning the Charlton-Dudley Regional School; a conference with the Superintendent of a small independent
school system (Harvard, Massachusetts); a study of the local real estate market; and discussions with the Fitch-
burg and Leominster Chambers of Commerce.
In addition, information was obtained from the Montachusett Regional Planning Commission, Montachusett
Regional Transit System, Center for Economic Development at the University of Massachusetts, and the Town
Clerk and Planning Board of Shirley. The Committee also requested that the Board of Selectmen reactivate the
School Housing Study Committee so additional information and figures will supplement our findings. At the time
of submission of this report, meetings are planned with the Lunenburg Board of Selectmen and Finance Committee,
and Shirley's Regional Study Committee. Visits are being set up with Nashoba Regional School, Groton-Dunstable
Regional School and the Tontasqua Regional High School. A fiscal study is in progress based on information from
many directions. The Town Treasurer is also being consulted.
We have been very interested in the study being done by Anderson-Nichols Consultants contracted by the
Planning Board. Our members have attended workshops and talked directly with Mr. Burk Ketchem. We will
continue attending subsequent workshops and review conclusions. We feel that their study and recommendations
will have a profound effect on our study.
This Committee is seeking a comprehensive view of regionalization. Furthermore, we are looking into alter-
nate ways to maintain an independent system in view of a declining school enrollment. We are not satisfied just
to accept figures and statistics, but are seeking a broad picture of the needs of the Lunenburg Schools, through
our independent research.
Regional School District Study Committee - left to right - Martha J. Moore, Edward J. Walkonen, Chairman Marion
M. Benson and William C. Bartlett. Not present when photo was taken was James A. Mann.
35
===PAGE 38===
School Housing Study Committee
This Committee was asked by the Board of Selectmen to work with the Regional School District Study Com-
mittee as a source of information on school population growth patterns that might be expected over the next
fifteen years. Data has been collected on Town birthrate and Town population over the last fifteen years. (Graphs
show school population.)
Enrollment has dropped from a 1974-1975 average of 2,164 to a 1981 - 1982 average (7 years later) of 1,722,
down 20%. This follows the birthrate trend adjusted for years from birth to grade in school. However, the school
population is consistently 30% to 35% above the number that births in Lunenburg would produce, caused by
population growth.
Data from the School Principals' records point to only a minor variation in the number coming into the schools
during each year compared to the number leaving. The number leaving Grades 9-12 is consistently higher than
those new students (15-24/per year). "Special Needs" students dropped moderately during this five year period.
Surveys of realtors and area planning commissions suggested area population growth to a moderate degree,
possibly 20% in fifteen years, but with ten years at least before the effect of Route I-190 might be felt. That is
borne out by the time delay felt from construction of Route I-495 by such towns as Harvard and Littleton, who are
now feeling these effects about a decade after highway completion.
This Committee sees a reduced usage factor in our buildings for the next five years, with no return to the
1975 usage for at least fifteen years.
School Housing Study Committee - left to right - Louis F. Nasti, James F. Gahl, Chairman Radcliffe P. Cheesman
and Herbert N. Hoffman. Not present when photo was taken were Carolyn W. Simsarian and Merton A. Penniman.

No-School Signals - 1982-83
The decision to close schools is made by the Superintendent of Schools after consulting with
the Highway Superintendent and the Police Department to determine the condition of the roads.
The practice is to close schools only in case of extreme weather conditions or unforeseen
emergency; therefore, when schools are in session on stormy days, parents are urged to exercise
their personal judgment as to the wisdom of sending their children to school.
No-School Signals for 1982-83
The following schedule of No-School Signals will be in effect for the current school year:
TWO SETS OF TWO (2) BLASTS ON THE HORN AT THE CENTRAL FIRE STATION INDICATES "NO
SCHOOL" AT THE FOLLOWING TIMES FOR THE GROUPS INDICATED -
6:30 AM
Grades 6 through 12
7:15 AM
Grades Kindergarten through 5
10:00 AM
PM Session of Kindergarten
Announcements will be carried on the regular broadcasts of radio stations WEIM and WFGL in
Fitchburg; WLMS in Leominster; as well as WSRS, WTAG and WAAF in Worcester; and WBZ, WHDH and WROR
in Boston, as promptly as the decision is made.
The cancelling of school sessions requires the completion of numerous separate telephone
calls as rapidly as possible. The crowding of telephone circuits can mean that the signal center
or bus contractor might not be notified on time. Parents can help by not allowing their children
to make unnecessary calls. Cooperation and understanding by all will be very much appreciated.
Annual Report of Superintendent of Schools
It is a privilege to submit my fifth annual report as
Superintendent of Schools in Lunenburg. This report covers the
year ending December 31, 1982.
As you read the reports of the other administrators, you
will receive a more detailed accounting of the events that have
taken place in the schools. The purpose of this report is to
summarize the total aspects of the Lunenburg Public Schools.
THE STUDENTS & STAFF:
I would be negligent in my duty if I did not point out the
quality of the students and the staff. We are, after all, no
better than the products we are given to work with. The qual-
ity of our school system is a direct result of the abilities of
- WILLIAM C. ALLARD, Ed. D. -
our students and the high regard that they and their parents
have for our staff. I have been most impressed, as I am sure
the entire town has, of the dedication of our teachers, custo-
dians, secretaries, cafeteria personnel, and administrators. Each and everyone of them strive
daily to provide a service contribution to the system that will reflect the daily growth of our
students. The responsibility that each of us feel in this daily task is enormous. We have SO
little time available to us to do our work that any time or effort that is lost is generally lost
forever. I am most appreciative to be working in an educational setting such as the Town of Lunen-
burg. The parents of this community continue to demonstrate their commitment to a quality educa-
tional experience by providing us with both a supportive home environment and a deep interest in
43
===PAGE 46===
their children's school activities. The entire town population shares in the tremendous pride
reflected in one of the best school systems in the state. All of us associated with the Lunenburg
Public Schools wish to express our appreciation for this support and interest.
THE PAST YEAR:
This past year has been a year of pleasant surprises and fulfillment of a number of ideas and
dreams. As a result of Proposition 21, we were forced to reduce our overall expenditures from a
budget of $4,138,975. to $3,506,167. or a total of 15.3% over the previous year. In that same year,
along with a reduction of $632,809., we had to purchase our goods and commodities at an inflation
rate of 12%. The history of how we accomplished this reduction and the effects that it had on the
system are well documented in previous town reports and newspaper articles. I will not elaborate
on that experience in this report. Suffice it to say that we did have drastic cuts and losses.
As a result of the town meeting in April 1982, the town voted on the recommendations of the
School Committee and the Finance Committee to grant the school department a budget of $3,793,599.
This reflected an increase of 8.4% over the previous year. I will elaborate on what that increase
provided for us during this present school year. In the meantime, we finished out the school year
under the immediate supervision of three new principals. Mrs. Concetta Verge completed her first
year as Principal of the Passios Elementary School. Even with reduced budgets, Mrs. Verge and her
staff are to be complimented on a successful year. Mrs. Carol Foley, Principal of the Turkey Hill
Middle School likewise completed her first year and introduced and initiated a number of new con-
cepts and ideas. She and her staff should be recognized for establishing a fine middle school
committed to providing the middle school student with a positive, individualized program. Dr. Ray
Peterson, Principal of the Lunenburg High School completed his first year with both a reduced staff
and a reduced administrative team. Again, Dr. Peterson and his staff are to be complimented on a
successful school year.
SEPTEMBER 1982 - DECEMBER 1982:
With the passage of the new budget in April, the School Committee and the administration pro-
ceeded to implement a number of changes that would reflect increased opportunities in programs and
realignments in staff and administration.
At the Elementary School we hired back a number of teachers that had been let go the previous
year. These teachers and some new people hired to replace individuals on leave-of-absence or
retiring brought our class sizes down to an average of twenty-five (25) per class. In addition, we
were able to introduce the following programs:
Elementary School - Pre-School Program
- Additional Resource Room
- Reduction of Class Sizes to Average of Twenty-five (25) Students
- Gifted and Talented Program (Grades 2, 3, 4, 5)
- Computer Assisted Instruction - Mini-computer
- Achievement Monitoring Program (Grades K-5) (Mathematics)
- Additional Part-time Secretary
- Library - Media Aide
- Small Group Classes (Grades 1 and 2)
- Physical Education Classes for all K-5 Students, Twice Per Week
- Structured Health Education Classes (Grades K-5)
- Classroom Language Intervention Program
- Retention of Existing Programs in Music, Art, Reading, Counseling
- Kindergarten Reading Readiness Program
- Kindergarten Readers (Accelerated)
- New Science Programs (K-5)
The new budget allowed us to implement the following at the Turkey Hill Middle School
- Structured Health Education Classes (Grades 6, 7, 8)
- Introduction of High School Credit Courses:
A. Foreign Languages (Latin, French, Spanish)
B. Mathematics (Algebra I)

- Increased Emphasis on Writing Skills
- Increased Emphasis on Basic Skills

All of these items required a great effort on the part of the School Committee and the admin-
istrators of our schools. They spent many hours reviewing plans and costs in order to achieve
such results. We owe them a great debt of gratitude for the time and effort they expended in
accomplishing everything that was previously listed.
ADMINISTRATIVE RE-ORGANIZATION:
With the loss of funds and the need to improve a K-12 curriculum revision in our school
system, the administration proposed and the School Committee accepted a complete revision of our
administration. The first point to be made about the re-organization is that all staff at each of
the three school levels is now under the direct supervision of the three principals. (See CHART #1)
Each principal is now charged with the responsibility of the evaluation, assignment and utili-
zation of all staff in each of the buildings. In addition, the student services such as special
education, guidance, counseling, discipline and athletics are the direct responsibility of the
principal to provide to each student within their buildings. Finally, all curriculum decisions are
to be the joint decision of the staff, principal and superintendent of schools.
At the Lunenburg High School the administration reorganization took into account the loss of
the department heads and greater control by the principal. (See CHART #2)
The attempt here was to introduce a new concept for administrative training. Rather than
life-time jobs as assistant principals, we are trying to train individuals to be administrators,
thus we limit administrative coordinator positions to three (3) years. In addition, these
45
===PAGE 48===
individuals continue in the teaching role part-time. Rather than have individual department heads
for each subject area as we have had in the past, we have consolidated these positions into
Instructional Program Coordinators heading up several departments. These individuals also have a
time limit to their positions (3 years) and have no evaluation of staff responsibilities.
CHART #1

We feel that at this time, in spite of the loss of several key positions such as an Assistant
Superintendent of Schools, Director of Reading and Language Arts, department heads, supervisors,
and directors, we have the monitoring and control over the curriculum and finances of the school
system. I will suggest, however, that we cannot eliminate or reorganize beyond our present struc-
ture without serious loss in these areas.
Presently we are providing the students and citizens with a quality educational system. There
are some losses because of reduced budgets. Our textbook situation is very tight, our athletic
program has suffered both losses in equipment purchases and programs, our building maintenance pro-
gram has shifted from long-range planning of repair and maintenance to simply repairing emergency
repairs and our capital purchasing has all but disappeared. We as an administration and a school
committee have placed our priority on staffing. We have moved our staffing percentage of the total
budget since 1980 from 69.7% to 80%. This means that there is less for everything else. Level
funding of any future school budgets will drastically cut staff and programs. I can no longer
46
===PAGE 49===
reorganize, reduce, maintain facilities, programs, transportation, class sizes, etc. by being pro-
vided the same monies as in previous years. Each year by contract we are required to fund teacher
salary increases. If we must absorb these raises with the same monies as provided in the preceed-
ing year, then we will have less money to provide services and equipment. Right now, I feel that
we have an excellent and well managed school system. I pray that we will be able to maintain that
control and quality by producing realistic budgets and continue to have town support for those
budgets.
