===PAGE 50===
We have introduced a number of changes in our financing of some of our programs in order to
make them self-sustaining. These have been implemented to improve the cost effectiveness of the
programs to the taxpayer. We have presently:
1. Implemented a user's fee for our athletic programs.
2. Implemented a building user fee.
3. Placed Adult Education on a self-sustaining basis.
4. Placed Driver Education on a self-sustaining basis.
5. Placed the Food Service Program on a self-sustaining basis.
6. Have relied on booster clubs, Music Aiders, and fund-raising events to financially
support our programs.
We must give credit to the clubs, booster clubs and volunteers that have come forth to pro-
vide the school system with both financial and human help during the past years. Without their
help, we would have had to eliminate a portion of a program or even the total program.
Last year, I established some goals that we would attempt to accomplish this school year.
Let me take these one at a time and respond to them.
1. A COMPLETE ANALYSIS OF OUR CURRICULUM WILL TAKE PLACE IN ORDER TO INSURE THAT WE ARE PER-
FORMING AN INTEGRATED, WELL-ARTICULATED K-12 CURRICULUM IN ALL AREAS.
It has long been my contention in the management of educational objectives that the prime
responsible person must be the school principal. It must be the principal who knows what
has to be done and who also has the authority and the knowledge base to do this. We have
in place the proper administrators and lines of authority to accomplish this goal. We are
already seeing great improvement in this area.
2. CONTINUED MAINTENANCE OF OUR FACILITIES IN SPITE OF DECREASED FINANCIAL RESOURCES.
This has been a most difficult area for me to accomplish. What generally has happened is
that we have dropped long-range planning and responded to emergency repairs. Our long-
range planning included replacement of doors, windows and the insulation of many of our
buildings. The School Committee has responded very well to our efforts of energy conser-
vation ideas but in most instances these have been accomplished with monies from other
accounts. My major concern for this goal is how long we will be able to maintain our
facilities in their present and proper condition responding only to emergency repairs.
3. A COMPLETE AND WELL DOCUMENTED STUDY OF THE POSSIBILITY OF REGIONALIZATION.
This has been answered by the creation of a regional study committee approved at the last
town meeting. This committee has been working long and hard to gather the necessary know-
ledge that will be required to make an intelligent response to this issue at the next town
meeting. I have both the faith and hope that they will have performed an outstanding job
to settle this very important concern.
4. A CAREFUL STUDY OF OUR ENROLLMENT PROJECTIONS AND THE IMPACT THAT THEY MIGHT HAVE RELATIVE
TO CURRICULUM AND FACILITIES.
This has been a constant monitoring on our part as administrators during the past year.
Whenever I could lower class sizes by hiring an additional teacher, I have done SO. Many
times this money could have been used to take care of some physical need for our buildings.
It has been most imperative for me to retain the proper staff so that our students will
have a better education.
5. CONTINUED EFFORTS TO PRODUCE A BUDGET THAT MEETS THE NEEDS OF THE SYSTEM TO PROVIDE
QUALITY EDUCATION TO EVERY INDIVIDUAL ENTRUSTED TO OUR EDUCATIONAL CARE.
I have always striven to provide the School Committee with a realistic budget based on
need. I have never played the game of asking for more than I felt was needed. In every
instance, I have looked for alternative staffing patterns, administrative reorganizations,
placing programs on a self-sustaining basis, etc., before I asked for money to maintain
the system. I will continue to do this for a better school system rather than "change
for change sake."
48
===PAGE 51===
6. CONTINUE EFFORTS TO KEEP OUR CITIZENS AWARE OF OUR CONCERNS, PROBLEMS AND SUCCESSES.
We have responded to this goal by implementing breakfast meetings with the superintendent,
newsletters from the schools, newspaper accounts of our accomplishments and activities,
and meetings in our schools. We are not always successful in getting into the media as
much news as would be necessary but this area has improved considerably over the last two
years.
7. CONTINUE EFFORTS TO SEEK ADDITIONAL FUNDS IN A TIME OF DECLINING RESOURCES.
This effort is usually behind the scenes working with various town groups, legislators
and lobbists. A variety of laws have been passed with the efforts of various groups such
as the MASS, MASC, etc. to allow the following:
- No longer having to incur the cost of transporting mentally retarded adults.
- No longer having to pay for transportation and tuitions for students attending post-high
school programs at WITI (Worcester Industrial Technical Institute) and Essex Community
College.
- Being allowed to implement users fees.
- Reimbursement for removal of asbestos.
8. INCREASE EFFORTS TO MAINTAIN AND ENCOURAGE A HIGHLY QUALIFIED STAFF.
I feel that we have made great strides in this area. As soon as I knew what my final
budget figures were after the town meeting in April, I submitted a list of teachers to be
recalled and new hires. Those who were recalled were outstanding teachers who had to be
released the year before. All of them had been well respected and competent teachers in
our system. The School Committee has been most supportive of my recommendations for
hiring back these teachers. We have also provided our staff with a number of workshops,
conference opportunities and consultant help to provide support and expertise to their
teaching role.
THE FUTURE:
The future should continue to look toward a continuation of quality. We have experienced
tremendous support from the citizens of this community for their schools. They continue to desire
a quality school system. With that in mind, I have established some goals which will have some
impact on our school system:
1. To continue to support the achievement monitoring system presently started at the elemen-
tary school and expand the concept throughout all grades.
2. Maintain a realistic budget presentation to the Town.
3. Maintain open communications with all town departments as to needs, goals, and joint
efforts.
4. Provide the administrators of our schools with the support and enthusiasm they SO justly
deserve.
5. Increase public awareness of what is taking place in our schools on a daily basis.
CONCLUSION:
In conclusion, I wish to pay special tribute to our School Committee. They must give many
hours of their private time to review reports and attend meetings. Each member of the committee
continues to devote this time and effort unselfishly. I have always been most impressed by the
quality of the individuals that serve as members of the school committee. It is testimony to
this community that they continue to elect such outstanding individuals to serve with me. Our
efforts are complimented by the high commitment that each member of the school committee has to
provide to make extremely important decisions affecting thousands of students. I personally thank
each member for this contribution and support. To my fellow administrators who on a daily basis
follow through with their goals and objectives and those of this community to provide the best
possible educational system. Their responsibility is awesome. I respect each of them for their
commitment and intelligence. We are most fortunate to have them available to us to perform this
49
===PAGE 52===
'most important responsibility. I wish to honor the teaching staff with high praise for their
efforts. Their daily call to "teacher" is what makes it all worthwhile for our students. To the
students, I thank them everyday for being what they are exciting; unpredicable, challenging,
loving, and above all, successful. Special thanks go to my very capable executive secretary,
"Ginny" Welch, who succeeds every day to make my work both pleasant and accurate. To Bob Okerman
whose love and devotion to this school system and to this town makes him far more valuable than
what we pay him. To the secretaries, cafeteria workers and custodians
their dedication and
outstanding performance keeps the "nuts and bolts" of the school system running smoothly. Finally,
to the citizens, both those who entrust their children to us and those who support us financially,
I thank them for the respect they accord us, Each day, we succeed because of their support and
dedication.

Lunenburg graduates continue to do well. 52% of our 1982 graduates went on to college; 36%
to four-year schools; 16% to two-year colleges. This is consistent with national trends affected
by the economy and technology. The number of our students entering military (8%) is also higher
(triple) the number of any of the previous five years. Our graduates entering the work force con-
tinue to be in demand by regional employers; 37 students (70%+ of those going to work) were placed
as a result of training, faculty placement, or work experience under LHS aegis.
Our intent as a school is to continue
to raise the qualifications and aspirations
of our students toward the most challenging
and rewarding experience following high
school; at the same time we will do more to
assist parents in planning to support these
aspirations against the economic and tech-
nological complexities of the times. Above
all, the first goal of the school is to
provide the highest possible general
intellectual education for every student
(not specialized training), which will
allow the most and best options for exper-
iences after graduation.

Report of Middle School Principal
- CAROL E. FOLEY -
- RICHARD J. GARVEY -
Principal
Administrative Assistant
INTRODUCTION:
The second year of dramatic fiscal restraints has caused increased pressure to deliver more
services to students with fewer resources available. This task has been accomplished here at the
middle school and has been done with great pride. We believe that our 460 students are receiving
the best possible range of educational opportunities we can provide with the resources we have
budgeted and with those that we have gained through fund-raising. Because of our reduced budget
(in comparison with two years ago) we have had to do more fund-raising. This places an extra
responsibility on the staff and students besides increasing financial demands on the community in
general. Fund-raising is important to us now, but we do not want it to detract from our educa-
tional program.
In the course of this one year
we have made many adjustments to
and assessments of our whole pro-
gram, both academic and special
area subjects, as well as the stu-
dent involvement at the middle
school.
TURKEY HILL MIDDLE SCHOOL

The core subjects consist of:
social studies, math, English,
science, reading and foreign lang-
uages. There is strong emphasis
on completion of basic skills
requirements and on pursuit of
excellence beyond the minimal
requirements. Our eighth grade
students have the opportunity to

CONCLUSION:
The middle school has had a full year and looks forward to the challenges of the next few
years. The educational challenges can be met if we have the community behind us. We cannot meet
the challenges if we are forced to reduce our staff and programs further. Our children will lose
the opportunities that we have been able to give up to this point. Visit the school and observe
what we offer.
Lastly, I wish to cite Dick Garvey, who again has been a most dependable and hardworking
assistant and teacher. Over the last year we have moved in a few new directions toward more stu-
dent involvement and computer education. Dick has been a strong supporter of these directions.
We both look forward to meeting the needs of our students and the community.

PRIMARY AND ELEMENTARY SCHOOL ENROLLMENT:
Enrollment in the Primary and Elementary Schools (1982-83) reflects a slight decline from
school year 1981-82. This year's total Primary and Elementary enrollment is 670 students. This
represents a decline of twenty-nine students, which is significantly less of a decline than we
have been sustaining over the past few years.

PRESERVATION OF ART, MUSIC AND PHYSICAL EDUCATION:
Despite the severe cuts of staff and material resources the Lunenburg School System has
sustained, we have attempted to preserve the programs in art, music, and physical education.
60
===PAGE 63===
Philosophically, the teachers, building administration, superintendent of schools, and school
committee believe that these three areas are integral components of the total curriculum. If we
are to educate the total child we must address the total curriculum.

A THUMBNAIL SKETCH OF THE ELEMENTARY AND PRIMARY SCHOOLS:
Having sustained serious staff cuts as a result of Proposition 21, the Elementary and Primary
Schools have spent the better part of school year 1981-82 attempting to rebuild the educational
program.
Teachers and other staff members have expended a great deal of time and effort in developing
educational alternatives in order to meet students needs. As a result of these efforts and strong
support from the Superintendent and School Committee we were able to regroup and develop programs
which would insure educational quality in the Lunenburg School System.

CLASSROOM LANGUAGE INTERVENTION PROGRAM:
Another area of weakness we found prevalent with a number of our students was language develop-
ment. As a matter of fact, many students began to show up as language deficient in grades three
and four, culminating in speech and writing deficiencies. Formerly, when this occurred, students
affected were put on Individualized Educational Plans and pulled out of class for special service
(therapy) with a speech and language specialist. By increasing the time of one of our formerly
part-time speech and language therapists and through the curriculum development efforts of another
speech and language therapist, Allan Wilson, and the building administration, a program was
developed in which language needs of all students are addressed in the regular classroom. Many
more students are reached through this program called CLIP (Classroom Language Intervention
Program) while regular classroom teachers who are involved in CLIP are receiving valuable in-
service.
62
===PAGE 65===
SMALL GROUP CLASSROOMS:
Often we find that very young
and create mas
children, of average to above average
intelligence, find it very difficult
to learn in the traditional classroom
housing twenty-five students or more.
They require more individual attention
and special instructional techniques
than the classroom teacher, dealing
with twenty-five students, can provide.
In order to give these particular
youngsters (identified by kindergarten
and first grade teachers as children
with additional needs) a good start,
we have established two SMALL GROUP
CLASSROOMS in grade one and two. These
classes house no more than eighteen
students. The classroom teacher
incorporates techniques which zero in
on individualized academic instruction
and encourage social/emotional adjust-
ment and growth. The goal of the SMALL
GROUP CLASSROOM is to bring each stu-
dent as close to his/her potential as
possible in order that he/she may
function in a full-sized third grade
class independently. One highly
skilled tutor spends a portion of each
day with the small group in grade one
and another highly skilled specialist
spends a portion of the day with the
second grade small group teacher and
class.
Mrs. Chivilo Works On Phonics With
Some of Her Small Group First Graders.
PRE-SCHOOL PROGRAM:
A rather unique program developed for a
very unique population is the SPECIAL NEEDS
Pre-Schoolers Are At Work On A
PRE-SCHOOL PROGRAM. This program was
Special Project With High School
developed to serve the needs of three, four,
Volunteer, Julie Lydon.
and five year olds who have difficulties
with speech and language. In the past, as
directed by law, children with difficulties in this area were brought into school to receive
therapy from school speech/language therapist or the therapist would visit the home of these
youngsters to provide service. In some cases pre-school children have had to be tuitioned out (at
school system expense) to private pre-schools. By creating our own pre-school we are now able to
coordinate all services to these youngsters, get maximum benefit out of the speech/language
therapist's time, educate our children in the community in which they live, and provide a head
start or preview to the regular curriculum they will be exposed to when they are ready to enter
Lunenburg's regular kindergarten class.
63
===PAGE 66===
LUNENBURG EXTRA ACHIEVEMENT PROGRAM:
Meeting the needs of children who are
experiencing difficulty with the regular
curriculum is only one focus of program
development at the Elementary level.
Another group of students who have special
needs is the group identified as gifted and
talented. These students generally possess
very high intelligence. They are often
abstract thinkers and are able to meet the
challenge and standards of the regular
curriculum with ease. These students
require enrichment and stimulation above
and beyond that which is met in the regular
classroom, in order to move closer to their
individual potentials. For that reason,
Project LEAP was created (Lunenburg Extra
Achievement Program). LEAP is a pull-out
program for gifted and/or talented students
Mr. Bregoli and Greg LaFreniere
in grades two through five. It is the
Define Activities for Project LEAP.
culmination of years of research and
committee work by teachers and admin-
istrators in Lunenburg under the
direction of Douglas Fleming. Paul
Bregoli is currently directing the
program in his capacity as half-time
fifth grade teacher and half-time
coordinator of the gifted and talented
program.

been developed and is currently being
piloted in the Primary School.

basis for about twenty to thirty minutes.
READING READINESS:
Regular curriculum needs also have been
addressed through program development. At
the Primary School a formal READING READI-
NESS PROGRAM has been established for all
kindergarten children. The goal of this
program is to prepare kindergarteners to
fit into more advanced reading levels at
grade one, to identify early those children
who might experience difficulty in reading,
and to develop study skills which they
might incorporate in their learning
Nathan Bourque and Jessica Johnson Work
repertoire.
On Readiness Reading Skills With
Mrs. Cosimi, Kindergarten Teacher.
64
===PAGE 67===
SCIENCE:
A great deal of attention has also been devoted to
the revamping of the science program, kindergarten through
grade five. The focus of the new science program is two-
fold. The content of the science program is addressed
through readings in the Holt science text, while process
is addressed through experiment/activities associated
with the Holt System. Concepts in the areas of Life,
Physical, and Earth Science have been delineated for
instruction at each grade level. All students are exposed
to the same scope of science concepts. Upon graduation
from the Elementary School all children will have a
unified and equal exposure to science. This not only
insures continuity and structure of the program but should
facilitate instruction and planning at the Middle School
level. Both children and students are excited and
enthusiastic about their involvement with this program.
HEALTH EDUCATION:
A component of most formal science programs is health
education. Ms. Barbara Jezak, health instructor at
Lunenburg, has implemented a HEALTH EDUCATION PROGRAM for
children in grades kindergarten through five. Ms. Jezak
is providing direct instruction for scheduled time periods
Billy Adams (4E) Experiments
during the year to each grade level dealing with specific
With Convex Lenses During
topics found in the health curriculum previously generated
Science Class.
by Ms. Jezak and a committee of teachers within the
school system.
Not only is the instructor able to highlight
areas of health education not normally covered in
the regular science program, but through example
(her actual teaching) is able to provide health
in-service (tips on materials, techniques, etc.)
to the regular classroom teacher.
ACHIEVEMENT MONITORING PROGRAM IN MATH:
Mathematics, one of the three basic skill
areas, has received extra attention this year
culminating in the development of AMP-MATH
(Achievement Monitoring Program in Mathematics).
In response to a slight dip in math scores, the
building administration and classroom teachers
re-examined the existing math curriculum and
materials, rewrote specific more pertinent math
objectives, have determined to focus instruction
Ms. Jezak Makes A Point During A
on those objectives, and formally monitor student
Health Education Lesson.
math achievement at six week intervals.
Incorporated in the monitoring component of the
math program are individual student printouts which delineate skills each child has mastered or
must work on and class summary sheets which facilitate planning for individual and small group
instruction. Through this process a more individualized, specific math program has been assured
for each student.
COMPUTER ASSISTED INSTRUCTION:
Since the decision was made to concentrate on improvement of math scores, an additional
educational innovation has been piloted at the Elementary School this year. It is COMPUTER
65
===PAGE 68===
ASSISTED INSTRUCTION (CAI). All grade four students are involved with CAI in mathematics on a
daily basis. Signficant improvement in fourth grade math test scores has already been noted.
Working through Merrimack Education Center, the school has leased terminals and computer pro-
grams from Instructional Systems, Incorporated of Englewood Cliffs, New Jersey, and Computer
Curriculum Corporation of Palo Alto, California. We have immediately available Courseware in
Mathematics, Reading, and Language Arts for the Elementary level. Also available is a selection
of Secondary, Adult and Special Interest Courseware.
Computer Assisted Instruction is a
classroom tool which gives students
daily practice in Basic Skills. It
provides individualized drill and
practice; instant feedback to the stu-
dent; individualized worksheets for each
student (Math Program); and printed
reports for each student. C. A. I.
frees the teacher from much of the
paperwork of planning and grading
practice lessons. The teacher can con-
centrate on teaching (and reteaching)
those skills at which the program shows
a student (or group of students) to be
lagging.
The enthusiasm shown by the stu-
dents is refreshing. Fourth grade stu-
dents give up a recess period every
other week (the teacher gives up her
"break") in order to get in the maximum
Mrs. Crowley Reviews AMP-MATH Printout
number of sessions possible. A number
With Chris Ann Barrett.
of fifth grade students (and their
teacher) come early two days a week to
work on math skills.
It is essential that school years kinder-
garten through grade five are structured to
insure a strong foundation in the basic skills
areas, to expose the children to the arts, to
introduce and educate in the content areas of
science and social studies, However, it is
most important to develop a need in each child
for the expansion of learning. The formative
years are the elementary years. It is the
goal of this level to build a strong foundation
to which the Middle School and High School
will later add.
ELEMENTARY SCHOOL GUIDANCE - A MUST:
We live in a complex and rapidly changing
society. There are many factors impacting on
family life such as frequent moves, separation
and divorce, financial problems, loss of jobs,
Ms. Morin Explains The Computer Functions
alcoholism, and the absence of extended family
To Her Fourth Grade Math Group.
support systems.
Therefore, the knowledge and skills of the Elementary School Guidance Counselor are extremely
important in helping children who increasingly encounter problems which cause confusion and
anxiety, as well as difficulty in adjusting to the school environment.
The counselor uses a knowledge of child growth and development, psychology, family systems,
learning theory, and community resources in working with children and parents, individually and in
groups. Skills in assessment of individual abilities and needs as well as consultation with
teachers, specialists, and administrators are also important components of the Elementary School
Guidance Counselor's service.

IN MEMORIAM
P. ROBERT CORMIER (May 31, 1938 - May 29, 1982)
We were all saddened by the death on Saturday, May 29, 1982 of Bob Cormier. Bob was graduated
from Assumption College in Worcester receiving an AB degree in June 1960. In September of the same
year he came to Lunenburg as a teacher of English and languages in our junior-senior high school.
During his tenure with us he served as language department head as well as school yearbook advisor.
He attended Notre Dame University graduate school in South Bend, Indiana and received a master's
degree in theology and linguistics from the University of Rennes, Brittany, France.
Due to his serious illness, he found it necessary to retire on March 31, 1980. In his letter
to the school committee and the superintendent, he indicated that "teaching for me was far more
than a livelihood--it was a life-style. Lunenburg served me well during my nineteen years, as I
hope I may have been of some service to Lunenburg. My world of teaching could not have been, and
would not have been possible, without the support of three persons who stand as pillars in my
school life--Mr. Richard Carey, Mrs. Helen Brockelman and Mr. Anthony Falcone. The first was
considerate in more ways than one in my behalf; the second, brought to me the life of the school
in so many ways; and the third, is the best friend a man is blessed with. For nineteen years,
Lunenburg was a major part of my life. There is a void which cannot be filled."
And there is a void for many of us with Bob's passing.
Bob is survived by his mother, Mrs. Viola "Billie" (Smith) Cormier, and two brothers, William
J. Cormier of Leominster and John M. Cormier of Lunenburg.
WILLIAM F. MORAN (December 19, 1900 - June 1, 1982)
Bill passed away on Tuesday, June 1, 1982 in Burbank
Hospital after a long illness.
He was a former owner and operator of Maple Hill Dairy
in Lunenburg and was employed from April 30, 1962 to December
31, 1970 as a custodian in the junior-senior high and middle
schools. He served as senior custodian at the Turkey Hill
Middle School from December 30, 1968 to his retirement. A
reception in his honor was held in the library of the middle
school on January 17, 1971 which was attended by school
personnel, family and friends, including former Superintendent
of Schools Ralph W. Brown. Robert Antonucci welcomed guests
and Richard Bishop was the master of ceremonies. A plaque
was presented to him by Walter Keeler (Chairman of the school
committee) honoring his years of dedicated service. Tribute
was also given him for his exemplary service by Superinten-
dent of Schools William R. Holland and Floyd Barney, Principal
of the middle school. A gold pendulum clock was given to him
by Harris Karis on behalf of the school faculty and a flower
and money centerpiece by Arthur Archambault, Supervisor of
Buildings & Grounds on behalf of the school department.
Bill is survived by two daughters, two sisters, five
grandchildrén and one great-grandchild.
68
===PAGE 71===
Retirements
during 1982
JOSEPH E. ALLEN - Effective Friday, May 21, 1982
Joe began his employment as a night custodian at Lunenburg High School effective May 18, 1977
under the CETA program. On September 12, 1977, the school department continued his employment
when his eligibility under CETA expired. On March 20, 1982 he submitted his intent to retire in
May after completing five years of service with the school department. Our best wishes went with
him for a happy and healthy retirement.
ANNE T. BLANCHARD - Effective Wednesday, June 30, 1982
Mrs. Blanchard began her employment as a general worker in the junior-senior high school
cafeteria effective September 1, 1969. On May 26, 1982 she submitted her intent to retire
effective in June after having served the cafeteria operation for a period of twelve years. Our
thanks, Anne, for a job well done.
JENNIE D. DAVIS - Effective Friday, November 5, 1982
"Jane" submitted her letter of intent to retire after having served the school system for
eight years as Payroll Secretary in the Central Office. This was a job she handled very
competently and thoroughly and her dedication to her position was very much appreciated. Many
happy and healthy years of retirement were wished her when she left.
RUTH R. HATHAWAY - Effective Friday, June 18, 1982
Mrs. Hathaway received her BS degree from Colby College, Maine in
1941. In the forties she worked in the children's department of the
public library in New Haven, Connecticut; in the fifties she tutored
students in Texas, New Mexico, Maryland, and Japan, her husband being a
member of the armed services. In the early sixties she taught in
Alamogardo, New Mexico and El Paso, Texas. She began in Lunenburg as a
teacher of grade two on January 4, 1965 to replace a teacher who had
been on leave and subsequently resigned. In recent years she has served
as one of our kindergarten teachers.
In her letter to the Superintendent indicating her intent to retire
after seventeen and one-half years of outstanding service to the children
of Lunenburg, "Bonnie" indicated that "I have many great memories of my
good years in Lunenburg schools and would like to express my gratitude
for the many opportunities and challenges offered me. I also want to
extend my fervent wish to the entire Lunenburg School System that some-
how, despite Proposition 21, Lunenburg schools may keep their reputation
for being one of the best school systems in the state, because of what
that means for our children."
We appreciate the many years that "Bonnie" provided excellent and high quality educational
experiences for the students of the Lunenburg Public Schools. Her commitment and dedication to
her job was exhibited by the outstanding performance on the part of her students and the great
respect that is held for her in this community for her efforts.
69
===PAGE 72===
JEAN E. HOBBS Effective Monday, June 21, 1982
At the time of her retirement, Jean was the Coordinating (Super-
vising) Nurse of the school department. She had served from 1968-1972
as intermittent part-time town and school nurse (elementary and middle
schools) and again beginning in January 1975 as part-time town and
school nurse (high school). She received her RN from The Memorial
Hospital School of Nursing in Worcester in 1947 and had been employed
in the Burbank Hospital prior to being employed by the Town of Lunen-
burg.
In accepting her intent to retire, the school committee "accepted
her retirement notification with regrets and wished to thank her for
the many years of service that she had provided the Lunenburg Public
Schools and its students. The dedication, the loyalty, and the commit-
ment that she exhibited in her involvement in the schools was highly
commendable and the committee wished to express its appreciation for
those efforts. We thank you for your participation and professional
dedication to your responsibilities during the course of your tenure
in the Lunenburg Public Schools."
GENEVIEVE T. NASS Effective Tuesday, June 15, 1982
"Gen" began work as a substitute in the school cafeteria back on October 24, 1961. Effec-
tive March 5, 1962, she became apermanent member of the cafeteria staff in the elementary school.
Due to the closing of the elementary school cafeteria effective September 1, 1966 she transferred
to the junior-senior high school. When the elementary school cafeteria was reactivated on March
31, 1969, she returned to that school and became the Cook/Manager, the position she held until
she retired in June 1982.
In her letter of intent to retire she indicated that "she had enjoyed working with the
which had been very happy ones for her."
children and would miss all her dear friends that she had made over her twenty years of service,
We thank her for her dedicated service in the performance of her duties. Many happy years
of retirement were wished her by all.
LILLIAN PENAN - Effective Friday, November 12, 1982
Mrs. Penan came to Lunenburg as a teacher of grade four on
September 1, 1964. Prior to that time she had had six plus years
of teaching experience in Warrenville, Connecticut and Brockton,
Massachusetts. She had also done substitute teaching in Fitchburg,
Lunenburg, and Dennisport, Massachusetts. She received her BS in
Education in 1942 from Bridgewater State College.
Lillian gave Lunenburg youngsters eighteen years of dedicated
and conscientious service for which we express our sincere thanks
and appreciation. We all wish her many happy years of retirement.

===PAGE 73===
SIRKKA W. SHINNER - Effective Friday, June 18, 1982
Mrs. Shinner attended Fitchburg High School and graduated from
Fitchburg State Teachers' College in 1941 receiving a BS in Education.
Sirkka taught at the Spaulding Memorial School in Townsend and the
Edgerly School in Fitchburg, before coming to Lunenburg in September
1959 when an additional grade two teacher was needed.
In her letter of intent to retire, she said "It is with some sad-
ness that 1 leave teaching, as it has always been such an important part
of my life. To have been associated with the dedicated and caring staff
of the elementary school, and to have served Lunenburg children these
past twenty-three years, has been, indeed, a privilege.'
It certainly was Lunenburg's gain to have had such a dedicated
teacher and we appreciate her commitment to her work and the efforts
that she expended for the benefit of the students of Lunenburg that had
the privilege to have Mrs. Shinner as their teacher.
BETHEL E. TAPPLY - Effective Friday, June 18, 1982
Mrs. Tapply began in September 1958 as a teacher of junior high
school English. She received her BS in Education from Fitchburg State
College in 1958 and her Master in Education from Boston University in
1963. Her previous teaching experience was in Lebanon, NH and South
Ashburnham, Massachusetts at grade six and junior high level. During
her twenty-four years of service with the Lunenburg Public Schools, she
served as a high school guidance counselor, as director of guidance,
and as the system's school psychologist for the past fifteen years.
A reception for Beth was held in the middle school library on
Thursday, June 17, 1982 hosted by the special education department in
appreciation for her devotion to her responsibilities and outstanding
dedication and quality of performance for the benefit of Lunenburg
students.
AILIE W. WILEY - Effective Friday, June 18, 1982
Mrs. Wiley attended Fitchburg High School and received her BS in
Education from Fitchburg State College in 1938. Prior to coming to
Lunenburg she taught over three years in Fairhaven High School and
substituted in the Leominster Public Schools. On September 1, 1964,
Ailie was hired as a grade six teacher.
Ailie indicated at the time of retirement that "I shall miss my
HEALTH
work, my colleagues and especially the children. 1 have had a very
happy experience teaching in Lunenburg and have considered it a privi-
lege to work in such a fine school system."
In accepting her intent to retire, the committee indicated their
sincere appreciation for the eighteen years that she has served faith-
fully with excellent demonstrated qualities for benefit of Lunenburg
students and commitment to her profession. We might add, Ailie is
still seen in the middle school helping out on a substitute basis.

remind us
We can make our lives

sublime,

behind us

of time."

MARY L. PADULA
Town Clerk
Treasurer
Tax Collector
FINAL REPORT
===PAGE 110===
Town Clerk, Treasurer, Tax Collector
It hardly seems possible that by the time this report
is printed / will have completed my 30th year as Lunen-
burg's Town Clerk, Town Treasurer, Tax Collector and
Accounting Officer. It is with both Joy and Sadness that
/ submit this, my final Annual Report, Joy - for the privilege
and pleasure of having for SO many years served the
Town that / love and the very special people who live
here, and Sadness - over the realization that / am about
to end this very significant chapter of my life.
Excerpts from Town Clerk,
MARY L. PADULA
Tax Collector,
Treasurer Reports
1953 - 1982:
1955. "The Treasurer makes annual debt and service payments and borrows money, and on December 1, 1955,
borrowed $985,000.00 as authorized by Article 13 of the warrant for the 1955 Annual Meeting. This bor-
rowing was done at the rate of 2.50%
1956, "Included in the 1957 Warrant is an article which calls for a sum of money to renovate and enlarge the
Town Office. This would be accomplished by eliminating the wall between the present office and a kitchen
which is no longer used. An increase in administrative funds has also been requested for 1957."
1958 "One of the most time consuming, yet rewarding, projects undertaken by this office is the preparation of
the Annual Town Report. The "Offset" process was employed in the printing of this year's report. This
means that the copy was furnished the printer ready for the camera. Although included in the cost of the
book, this year, is the purchase of an IBM Executive Model electric typewriter, a considerable savings
should be realized in the future as a result of preparing the book in this manner."
1960. "Although unable to answer every one of the many and diversified questions which were asked at the
Town Office, such as, 'What is the proper attire for the roller skating rink?', 'What is the best method of
disposing of swarming bees?', 'What do you do with dead skunks in the road?', and 'Is deer meat which
has remained in a freezer for two and one-half years still edible?', we were able to ascertain that a permit
is required to keep a racoon, did endeavor to supply the names of President-elect Kennedy's newly ap-
pointed cabinet members, and trust that, in general, we were able to satisfactorily dispose of the majority
of the problems with which we were confronted."
108
===PAGE 111===
1961 "In the ten year period from 1951 to 1961 the volume of work in the Town Office has more than doubled.
The Town Clerk is grateful to Mrs. Sylvia Oksanen and Miss Joyce White, who work in the Town Office on
a full time basis, and to Mrs. Ine Pihlaja, who is employed part time, for their loyal, conscientious, and
expeditious handling of the numerous and varied tasks which they are called upon to perform. However,
in spite of our best efforts, we find that we cannot keep pace with increased work loads and have, there-
fore, requested the services of an additional clerk commencing in March 1962.'
1962. "The image of Massachusetts Town Government has been described as a rural settlement with a white
town hall, staffed principally by a town clerk and numerous part-time boards, several colonial-style churches
abutting an elm-dotted common, a small commercial center where business is transacted on a first-name
basis in a rather leisurely, friendly manner, and a long tradition of local participation in town affairs. Al-
though, fortunately, this picture is an accurate image of the outward appearance of our Town, a closer
look at the "balance sheet" discloses that Lunenburg's development, through the years, into an urban
community performing many of the same municipal functions as far larger towns do elsewhere, has been
masqued by its colonial facade.
Realizing that a form of government which was adequate and suitable for the needs of people twenty-five
years ago could not cope with today's municipal problems without considerable modification, we have
endeavored through continuous organizational improvements, more effective utilization of personnel and
equipment, and the up-dating of facilities, to reshape the various functions of the Town Office so as to
meet new challenges and new situations and yet preserve much of the time-honored traditions and customs."
1965 "The unusually large number of independent boards, commissions and departments which characterize
the administrative structure of most Massachusetts Towns has been described as an administrative
weakness. In order to overcome this weakness more and more towns are consolidating administrative
service functions into one department for efficiency of operation, more effective use of personnel and as
a convenience to the townspeople. In Lunenburg the administrative function of the Board of Selectmen,
Board of Health, Zoning Board and Licensing Authority are combined with those of the Town Clerk, Tax
Collector, Treasurer and Registrars of Voters."
1966 "Although we must certainly recognize the merit in and necessity for rigid control of municipal financing
we might well criticize the State system of accounting. It is ironic that the State prescribes a system of
accounting wherein we commence spending monies in January which are 1. voted the following March;
2. raised by property taxes which are billed the following June; and 3. actually received mainly in the last
three months of the year. As an example, $695,569 of the $989,593 in 1966 real estate taxes assessed
were received after October 1, 1966 even though this is the bulk of the money with which we are supposed
to run our Government during the entire calendar year 1966. This means that the Treasurer must borrow
money in anticipation of the taxes and other anticipated revenue until such revenue is received."
1972 "In 1969, the State Legislature passed what has come to be known as "The Fiscal Cycle Bill", which has
far-reaching effects on every city and town in the Commonwealth. The original Bill was so poorly conceived
that in 1969 and again in 1970 the Legislature postponed action on it. In 1971, 23 pages of corrections
were drafted, and the Bill was once more placed before the Legislature, whereupon in spite of protests
by the State Division of Accounts, who were much concerned over the errors, omissions and inconsistencies
of even the corrected version, it was passed. Further amendments and corrections are being worked on
even as the Bill goes into effect.
One of the main features of this legislation is to effect twice-a-year billing of Real Estate Taxes. In the 18
month transitional year, two part bills will be mailed in June for the entire 18 month period, but only 2/3 of
the bill, or 12 months worth, will become overdue on November 1. The last 1/3 will become overdue on
April 1 (to be changed to May 1). In subsequent years 12 month bills will be mailed in June, with 1/2 be-
coming overdue on November 1 and 1/2 becoming overdue on May 1. This means that the banks will be
paying over tax monies twice a year, in October and April. The net effect on the taxpayer is that he will be
paying the tax for the first half of a calendar year six months earlier than heretofore. The advantage to the
Town which this "speedup" represents is difficult to evaluate in advance. Theoretically the extra payment
will save the Town money, because we will not have to borrow as much in anticipation of revenue, and
therefore will pay out less interest. However, the increased costs of extra clerical services involved in the
duplicate billing process will probably equal or even exceed the savings on interest."
109
===PAGE 112===
1974. "Motor Vehicle Excise billing continues to be a source of annoyance to Taxpayers and Tax Officials alike.
An examination of excise bills issued by the State Department of Motor Vehicles has shown that approxi-
mately 25% of them are in error. The resulting confusion and inconvenience to vehicle owners and to the
municipality which processes these bills is immeasurable, as is the cost involved. City and Town officials
throughout the State are calling for a complete revision and correction of the system presently employed
by the Registry of Motor Vehicles."
1977 "Effective cash management is generally recognized as essential to good fiscal management. This is par-
ticularly true as mounting costs have placed ever-increasing pressures on local revenues, and we must
find ways to meet increased costs while placing a minimum additional burden on the taxpayer. Many of
the policies and procedures that are only now being introduced to many local governments for use in
investing so-called "idle cash" (money that is available in the treasury but is not needed for current day-
to-day financial transactions) have been employed in our Town for many years, and in Fiscal 1976 and
Fiscal 1977, for instance, resulted in total interest earnings in excess of $125,000. Even so, the Treasurer/
Collector's office is in the process of reviewing and improving collection, deposit and disbursement pro-
cedures to assure even greater availability of funds to meet cash needs and for investment purposes."
1980 "It is assumed that Lunenburg taxpayers, by their overwhelming vote on the subject, do want and expect
Lunenburg officials to make every attempt to comply with the 2½ Mandate, and do want and expect to be
provided with the very best Government possible within the constraints of Proposition 2½.
In recent years, new emphasis has been placed on the need for improved Financial Management in view
of what is deemed to be the precarious financial condition of some Massachusetts cities and towns. This
year, even more emphasis is being placed on Financial Management because of Proposition 2½ and
there is a growing concern that cuts in financial budgets will result in loss of income that will far surpass the
small amount of savings realized by reducing financial office staffs. At present, Lunenburg's tax bills go
out on schedule, tax collections are right up to the minute, our accounts receivable do not include any
back property taxes, our tax titles are foreclosed as quickly as possible and we cash manage to the hilt."
1981 "In spite of the constraints of Proposition 2½, Lunenburg is extremely healthy. We ended Fiscal Year '81
with an unrestricted fund balance of $520,155 and no uncollected Real Estate taxes. Our Fiscal Year '82
tax bills were mailed on time and Real Estate taxes were received on or before the usual November 1
deadline. We have not had to borrow in anticipation of revenue since 1973. We were able to weather the
first year of Proposition 2½ without any appreciable reduction in services and, even with a substantial
reduction in our teaching staff, our present pupil/teacher-supervisor ratio is under seventeen. We do not
at the present time anticipate further reductions in staff in Fiscal Year '83 and could inflate current oper-
ating budgets an estimated seven percent and still absorb a $100,000 decrease in State Aid. Current
prognostications by State Legislators notwithstanding, we are confident that State Aid will not be cut in
1982 for that type of thing simple does not happen in the State of Massachusetts during an Election Year."
1982. As predicted, in 1982 (an election year) the State did indeed come through with the additional aid and the
Town was able to carry on in Fiscal 1983 without any further reduction in services. In my opinion, Fiscal
year 1984 is, however, going to be tough - It may be time to call on the voters at the polls to effect the
"override provision" of Proposition 2½. The F.Y. 1983 tax rate was $19.40, or $5.60 under the $25.00
permitted by Prop. 2½. What "Prop. 2½" says is that it may be necessary for a city or town to increase
its tax levy more than 2½ percent a year. In fact If inflation continues to average more than 2½% a year
and we never voted to override, eventually we would have no real estate taxes and neither would we have
any town government. That is to day, it was not the intent of this legislation to never increase spending more
than 21/2% over the previous year, it was instead the intent of "Prop. 2½" to allow the voters at the polls to
authorize any such spending, rather than leaving it up to a smaller nucleus of people at Town Meeting.
Accordingly, the April Ballot will contain a question which asks you to authorize the expenditure of an
additional $93,387. A "yes" vote will add approximately 50 cents to the F.Y. '84 tax rate, and will cost
the average taxpayer approximately $35.00. It appears to me that this is a reasonable request and that
the $93,387 is needed to maintain last year's level of service.
110

Vital Statistics - 1982
Following are those records of Births, Marriages and Deaths that either occurred in our Town or records which were forwarded to this
Town from other municipalities in the State. Lunenburg residents are urged to notify the Town Clerk if in-State records have not been received
and recorded. If a Birth, Marriage or Death occurred outside the State, the same may be recorded in the Lunenburg Town Clerk's office if
certified records are presented. In some cases, the Town Clerk's records are supplemented by records of burials which appear elsewhere in
this report. In order for these records to also be included in the official Vital Statistics records, the family of the deceased should present a
certified copy of the death certificate to the Town Clerk. In accordance with recent changes in the General Laws, marriages which occur here
but relate to licenses which were obtained elsewhere in the State are no longer included in our official Town Records.

Excerpts From Special Town Meeting Minutes
January 18, 1982
ARTICLE 1. To see if the Town will vote to petition the Great and General Court of Massachusetts to enact a law for recall elections
in the Town of Lunenburg, or take any action in relation thereto. VOTED THAT THE TOWN DIRECT THE SELECTMEN TO PETITION THE
GREAT AND GENERAL COURT OF MASSACHUSETTS FOR PASSAGE OF THE FOLLOWING PROPOSED ACT AUTHORIZING RECALL
ELECTIONS IN THE TOWN OF LUNENBURG:
AN ACT PROVIDING FOR RECALL ELECTIONS IN THE TOWN OF LUNENBURG
SECTION 1. Any holder of an elected office in the Town of Lunenburg may be recalled therefrom by the registered voters of the town
as herein provided.
SECTION 2. Any one hundred registered voters of the town may initiate a recall petition by filing with the town clerk an affidavit con-
taining the name of the officer sought to be recalled and a statement of the grounds for recall. The town clerk shall thereupon deliver to said
voters making the affidavit copies of petition blanks demanding such recall, copies of which printed forms he shall keep available. The blanks
shall be issued by the town clerk, with his signature and official seal attached thereto. They shall be dated, shall be addressed to the select-
men and shall contain the names of all the persons to whom they are issued, the name of the person whose recall is sought, the grounds
of recall as stated in the affidavit, and shall demand the election of a successor in the said office. A copy of the petition shall be entered in
a record book to be kept in the office of the town clerk. The recall petition shall be returned and filed with the town clerk within twenty days
after the filing of the affidavit, and shall have been signed by at least twenty per cent of the registered voters of the town, who shall add to
their signatures the street and number, if any, of their residences.
The town clerk shall within twenty-four (24) hours of receipt submit the petition to the registrars of voters in the town, and the registrars
shall forthwith certify thereon the number of signatures which are names of registered voters of the town.
SECTION 3. If the petition shall be found and certified by the town clerk to be sufficient he shall submit the same with his certificate
to the selectmen without delay, and the selectmen shall forthwith give written notice of the receipt of the certificate to the officer sought to
be recalled and shall, if the officer does not resign within five days thereafter, order an election to be held on a date fixed by them not less
than twenty-five nor more than thirty-five days after the date of the town clerk's certificate that a sufficient petition has been filed; provided
however that if any other town election is to occur within sixty days after the date of the certificate the selectmen shall postpone the holding
of the recall election to the date of such other election. No person shall be subject to recall if his term of office expires within sixty days of
the certificate. If a vacancy occurs in said office after a recall election has been ordered, the election shall nevertheless proceed as provided
in this section.
SECTION 4. Any officer sought to be removed may be a candidate to succeed himself, and unless he requests otherwise in writing,
the town clerk shall place his name on the ballot without nomination. The nomination of other candidates, the publication of the warrant for
the removal election, and the conduct of the same, shall all be in accordance with the provisions of law relating to elections, unless otherwise
provided in this section.
SECTION 5. The incumbent shall continue to perform the duties of his office until the recall election. If then reelected, he shall continue
in office for the remainder of his unexpired term subject to recall as before, except as provided in this section. If not reelected in the recall
election, he shall be deemed removed upon the qualification of his successor, who shall hold office during the unexpired term. If the successor
fails to qualify within five days after receiving notification of his election, the incumbent shall thereupon be deemed removed and the office
vacant.
149
===PAGE 152===
SECTION 6. Ballots used in a recall election shall submit the following propositions in the order indicated:
For the recall of (name of officers).
Against the recall of (name of officers).
Immediately at the right of each proposition there shall be a square in which the voter, by making a cross mark (X) may vote for either of the
said propositions. Under the propositions shall appear the word "Candidates", the directions to the voters required by Section 42 of Chapter
54 of the General Laws, and beneath this the names of candidates nominated in accordance with the provisions of law relating to elections.
If two-thirds of the votes cast upon the question of recall is in the affirmative, the candidate receiving the highest number of votes shall be
declared elected. If more than one-third of the votes on the question are in the negative, the ballots for candidates need not be counted.
SECTION 7. No recall petition shall be filed against an officer within ninety days after he takes office, nor, in the case of an officer
subjected to a recall election and not recalled thereby, until at least ninety days after the election at which his recall was submitted to the
voters of the town.
SECTION 8. No person who has been recalled from an office, or who has resigned from office while recall proceedings were pending
against him, shall be appointed to any town office within two years after such recall or such resignation.
SECTION 9. This act shall be submitted for acceptance to the voters of the Town of Lunenburg at the next Annual Town Election or
a Special Town Election, whichever occurs first, in the form of the following question which shall be placed on the official ballot at such
election: "Shall an act passed by the General Court in the year nineteen hundred and eighty-two entitled 'An act providing for recall elections
in the Town of Lunenburg', be accepted?" If a majority of the votes cast in answer to said question is in the affirmative, this act shall take
effect, but not otherwise.
ARTICLE 2. To see if the Town will vote to hear a report and recommendations of the Ad Hoc Gypsy Moth Committee which was ap-
pointed by the Board of Selectmen, or act anything thereon. VOTED.
ARTICLE 3. To see if the Town will vote to appropriate and transfer from any available funds, a sum or sums of money for the purpose
of a Gypsy Moth Control Program including but not limited to aerial and/or roadside spraying and further authorize the Board of Selectmen
to take any action in furtherance of said program including the acceptance and expenditure of any additional funds received from any
Federal and/or State Agency, or act anything thereon. MOTION DEFEATED.
ARTICLE 4. To see if the Town will vote to appropriate and transfer from the Burial Rights Account the sum of $3,400.00, or some
other sum or sums, for purchase of a riding tractor mower and vacuum for use by the Cemetery Department, or act anything thereon. VOTED.

QUESTION NO. 1 PROPOSED AMENDMENT TO THE CONSTITUTION
Do you approve of the adoption of an amendment to the constitution summarized below, which was approved by the General Court in
joint sessions of the House of Representatives and the Senate on July 2, 1980 by a vote of 171-4, and on June 21, 1982 by a vote of 144-44?
SUMMARY - The proposed constitutional amendment would remove the present constitutional prohibition against the use of public
funds to aid or maintain private primary or secondary schools.
It would permit the Commonwealth, cities and towns to make public funds available to pupils attending private primary and secondary
schools in the form of either aid, materials or services subject, however, to three specific limitations. First, the private school could not be
one that discriminates on the basis of race or color in its admission requirements. Second, the grant of aid must be consistent with the First
Amendment to the United States Constitution which guarantees the free exercise of religion and prohibits the establishment of religion.
Third, individual pupils would have to request the aid, materials or services. In addition to these three specific limitations, the amendment
would authorize the legislature to enact other laws imposing conditions or restrictions on the grant of public aid, materials or services.
The proposal would also change the state constitution to allow public money to be spent to aid infirmaries, hospitals, charitable or
religious undertakings if they are either publicly owned or under the control of public officials. The state constitution now prohibits such
spending unless these institutions are both publicly owned and under the control of public officials.
VOTE:
YES- 944
NO- 2556
BLANKS - 195
QUESTION NO. 2- PROPOSED AMENDMENT TO THE CONSTITUTION
Do you approve of the adoption of an amendment to the constitution summarized below, which was approved by the General Court in
joint sessions of the House of Representatives and the Senate on September 19, 1980 by a vote of 123-63, and on June 21, 1982 by a vote
of 125-62?
SUMMARY - The proposed constitutional amendment would allow the legislature to enact laws authorizing the state courts to impose
the death penalty on the conviction of crimes to be specified by law. The proposed amendment would provide that no provision of the state
constitution may in the future be construed as prohibiting the imposition of the punishment of death.
VOTE:
YES- 2316
NO- 1190
BLANKS - 189
QUESTION NO. 3- LAW PROPOSED BY INITIATIVE PETITION
7, 1982?
Do you approve of a law summarized below, on which no vote was taken by the House of Representatives or the Senate before July
SUMMARY - The proposed law would require that before the construction or operation of any new nuclear power plant or low-level
radioactive waste storage or disposal facility in the Commonwealth, the legislature must make certain findings and a majority of voters must
approve the new facility at a statewide election.
Before the question of building a new nuclear power plant could be submitted to the voters, the legislature would have to find that (1)
the proposed facility is the best means for meeting energy needs based on certain economic, safety, environmental and social considerations;
(2) a federally-licensed facility exists for the disposal of the high-level radioactive waste that would be generated: (3) an approved emergency
preparedness plan has been developed; (4) radioactive pollution standards have been promulgated; and (5) a demonstrated, federally-ap-
proved technology exists for decommissioning the proposed power plant.
152
===PAGE 155===
Before the question of building and operating a low-level radioactive waste storage or disposal facility or of entering into an agreement
with another state to build and operate such a facility in Massachusetts could be submitted to the voters, the legislature would have to find
that the technology and site designated for the proposed facility are the best available based on certain economic, safety, environmental
and social considerations. The legislature would also have to find that the obligations imposed on Massachusetts by any interstate agreement
were no greater than those imposed on any other state.
The proposal would not apply to a facility which had obtained all necessary government approvals before August 5, 1981, nor to any
facility for disposal or storage of radioactive wastes from medical or bio-research applications in Massachusetts.
VOTE:
YES - 2354
NO - 1131
BLANKS - 210
QUESTION NO. 4 - REFERENDUM ON AN EXISTING LAW
Do you approve of a law summarized below, which was approved by the House of Representatives on November 10, 1981 by a vote of
108-49, and by the Senate on November 16, 1981 by a vote of 29-10?
SUMMARY - The law requires that a refundable deposit be paid for certain beverage containers sold in Massachusetts.
Beverage containers of less than 32 ounces must have a refund value of at least five cents and larger containers a refund value of at
least ten cents. This requirement applies to nonbiodegradable containers of carbonated soft drinks, mineral water, beer and other malt beverages,
BUT NOT TO CONTAINERS OF OTHER ALCOHOLIC BEVERAGES, DAIRY PRODUCTS, NATURAL FRUIT JUICES OR WINE. All beverage
containers subject to deposit must clearly indicate the refund value on the container.
The deposit is paid by the consumer upon purchase and must be refunded when the consumer returns the empty container to a proper
dealer or redemption center, SO long as the container does not contain any material different from its normal contents. Dealers and distributors
are also subject to the same deposit and refund on the beverage containers they handle, and are also entitled to a handling fee of at least one
cent per containers.
No containers can be sold in the state if they are joined together by plastic rings or any other devices that cannot be broken down by
light or bacteria.
The law provides a bottler a reduction in corporate excise tax of one-tenth of one cent for each reusable beverage container which the
bottler sells in the first three months of 1983. The law provides for additional unemployment benefits and, if the Legislature appropriates the
funds, a job retraining program for employees of bottlers, canners, or manufacturers of beverage containers who lose their jobs as a result
of this law.
The law takes effect on January 17, 1983.
VOTE:
YES - 2182
NO - 1409
BLANKS - 104

ARTICLE 5. To see if the Town will vote to amend Section VI of its Protective By-law by adding thereto floodplain district delineation
and use regulations in order to protect the public health, safety and general welfare, to protect human life and property from the hazards of
periodic flooding, to preserve the natural flood control characteristics, and the flood storage capacity of the flood plain, and to preserve and
maintain the ground water table and water recharge areas within the flood plain, by adding a new sub-section to be known as sub-section E,
to read as presented to the Town Meeting in the text of the handout supplied, or act anything thereton.
VOTED that the Town amend Section VI of the Protective By-law by adding after Section VI D, a new section to be known as Section VI E.
Flood Plain District, as follows:
E. Flood Plain District
1. Purpose
The purposes of the Flood Plain District are to protect the public health, safety, and general welfare, to protect human life and property
from hazards of periodic flooding, to preserve the natural flood control characteristics, and the flood storage capacity of the floodplain,
and to preserve and maintain the ground water table and water recharge areas within the flood plain.
2. District Delineation
The general boundaries of the Flood Plain District are shown on the Lunenburg Flood Insurance Rate Map (FIRM), dated June 15, 1982,
as Zones A, A 1-30 to indicate the 100 year flood plain. The exact boundaries of the District are defined by the 100 year water surface
elevations shown on the FIRM and further defined by the Flood Profiles contained in the Flood Insurance Study, dated December 15,
1981. The floodway boundaries are delineated on the Lunenburg Flood Boundary Floodway Map (FBF,) dated June 15, 1982 and further
defined by the Floodway Data Tables contained in the Flood Insurance Study. These two maps as well as the accompanying Study
are incorporated herein by reference and are on file with the Town Clerk, Planning Board, Building Inspector and Board of Selectmen.
Within Zone A, where the 100 year flood elevation is not provided on the FIRM, the developer/applicant shall obtain any existing flood
elevation data and it shall be reviewed by the Planning Board. If the data is sufficiently detailed and accurate, it shall be relied upon to
require compliance with this by-law and the State Building Code.
3. Use Regulations
The Flood Plain District is established as an overlay district to all other districts. All development, including structural and non-structural
activities, whether permitted by right or by special permit must be in compliance with Chapter 131, Section 40 of the Massachusetts
General Laws and with the requirements of the Massachusetts State Building Code pertaining to construction in the flood plains
(currently Section 744).
A. Permitted Uses
The following uses of low flood damage potential and causing no obstructions to flood flows shall be allowed provided they are
permitted in the underlying district and they do not require structures, fill, or storage of materials or equipment:
1) Agricultural uses such as farming, grazing, truck farming, horticulture, etc.
2) Forestry and nursery uses.
3) Outdoor recreational uses, including fishing, boating, play areas, etc.
4) Conservation of water, plants, wildlife.
5) Wildlife management areas, foot, bicycle, and/or horse paths.
6) Temporary non-residential structures used in connection with fishing, growing, harvesting, storage, or sale of crops raised
on the premises.
7) Buildings lawfully existing prior to the adoption of these provisions.
B. Special Permits
No structure or building shall be erected, constructed, substantially improved, or otherwise created or moved; no earth or other
materials dumped, filled, excavated, or transferred, unless a special permit is granted by the Planning Board. Said Board may
issue a special permit hereunder (subject to other applicable provisions of this by-law) if the application is compliant with the
following provisions:
1) The proposed use shall comply in all respects with the provisions of the underlying District, and
2) Within 10 days of receipt of the application, the Board shall transmit one copy of the development plan to the Conservation
Commission, Board of Health, and Building Inspector. Final action shall not be taken until reports have been received from
the above Boards or until 35 days have elapsed, and
156
===PAGE 159===
3) All encroachments, including fill, new construction, substantial improvements to existing structures, and other development
are prohibited unless certification by a registered professional engineer is provided by the applicant demonstrating that
such encroachment shall not result in any increase in flood levels during the occurrence of the 100 year flood, and
4) The Board may specify such additional requirements and conditions it finds necessary to protect the health, safety, and
welfare of the public and the occupants of the proposed use.

ARTICLE 23. To see If the Town will vote to create a regional School District Study Committee of three members, one of whom shall
be appointed by the School Committee, one of whom shall be appointed by the Planning Board and one of whom shall be appointed by the
Moderator, such Committee to study the advisability of establishing a Regional School District with the Town of Shirley, such study to include:
(1) The development of school enrollment projections of both communities based on the consideration of all possible influences thereon;
(2) The consideration of organization, operation and control of such a Regional School District including the use, sale and lease or con-
struction of school facilities; (3) The projected financial impact on our community including such matters as potential changes in existing
school aid formulas and any indirect influences on such financial impact such as loss of local control of our educational costs; (4) The con-
sideration of any alternatives to regionalization such as tultion basis students, and; (5) The consideration of any other direct or indirect aspects
of such action including the quality of education and the overall long term social and economic impact on the Town of Lunenburg, such com-
mittee to report its findings and recommendation to the next Annual Town Meeting including a recommendation as to whether or not a
Regional School District Committee should be appointed pursuant to Massachusetts General Laws, Chapter 71, Section 14 and that the sum
of $2,500 be appropriated for the use of such study committee, or act anything thereon. (Finance Committee recommends $2,500 appropri-
ation). VOTED THAT THE TOWN CREATE A REGIONAL SCHOOL DISTRICT STUDY COMMITTEE OF FIVE MEMBERS, ONE OF WHOM
SHALL BE APPOINTED BY THE SCHOOL COMMITTEE, ONE OF WHOM SHALL BE APPOINTED BY THE PLANNING BOARD AND THREE OF
WHOM SHALL BE APPOINTED BY THE MODERATOR AND SELECTMEN, SUCH COMMITTEE TO STUDY THE ADVISABILITY OF ESTAB-
LISHING A REGIONAL SCHOOL DISTRICT WITH THE TOWN OF SHIRLEY, SUCH STUDY TO INCLUDE: (1) THE DEVELOPMENT OF SCHOOL
ENROLLMENT PROJECTIONS OF BOTH COMMUNITIES BASED ON THE CONSIDERATION OF ALL POSSIBLE INFLUENCES THEREON;
(2) THE CONSIDERATION OF ORGANIZATION, OPERATION AND CONTROL OF SUCH A REGIONAL SCHOOL DISTRICT INCLUDING THE
USE, SALE, AND LEASE OR CONSTRUCTION OF SCHOOL FACILITIES; (3) THE PROJECTED FINANCIAL IMPACT ON OUR COMMUNITY
INCLUDING SUCH MATTERS AS POTENTIAL CHANGES IN EXISTING SCHOOL AID FORMULAS AND ANY INDIRECT INFLUENCES ON
SUCH FINANCIAL IMPACT SUCH AS LOSS OF LOCAL CONTROL OF OUR EDUCATION COSTS; (4) THE CONSIDERATION OF ANY AL-
TERNATIVES TO REGIONALIZATION SUCH AS TUITION BASIS STUDENTS, AND; (5) THE CONSIDERATION OF ANY OTHER DIRECT OR
INDIRECT ASPECTS OF SUCH ACTION INCLUDING THE QUALITY OF EDUCATION AND THE OVERALL LONG TERM SOCIAL AND ECO-
NOMIC IMPACT ON THE TOWN OF LUNENBURG, SUCH COMMITTEE TO REPORT ITS FINDINGS AND RECOMMENDATIONS NOT LATER
THAN TWO WEEKS BEFORE THE ANNUAL TOWN MEETING. COMMITTEE REPORT ITS FINDINGS TO A PUBLIC MEETING INCLUDING
A RECOMMENDATION AS TO WHETHER OR NOT A REGIONAL SCHOOL DISTRICT COMMITTEE SHOULD BE APPOINTED PURSUANT TO
MASSACHUSETTS GENERAL LAWS, CHAPTER 71, SECTION 14 AND THAT THE SUM OF $2,500 BE RAISED BY TAXATION AND APPRO-
PRIATE FROM AVAILABLE FUNDS.

Cover: The Unitarian Church built in 1830, originally located on the Center Common, was purchased
by the Town in 1867 for a town hall. The building was moved to its present location on Main-
Street. The town clock was given to the Town by Jacob H. Fairbanks in 1894.
